> • that it is more probable than not that one of the commission’s counterfactuals will come to pass if the proposed acquisition does not proceed; and

> • that there is a real chance that, if the proposed acquisition does proceed, that would result in a substantial lessening of competition compared to the scenario in which one of those counterfactuals comes to pass.

[1271] So his Honour concluded that the counterfactual had to be proved on the balance of probabilities.

[1272] Now it was not strictly necessary for the Full Court to come to a concluded view on whether a double standard applied concerning the counterfactual and proving a substantial lessening of competition. That was because the primary judge found that even if the ACCC was only required to establish a real chance that its counterfactual would come to pass, it had not satisfied that lower standard in any event. But Yates J doubted the existence of a double standard for establishing the counterfactual and for proving a substantial lessening of competition and stated (at [227] to [229]):

> If one accepts that the starting point is to draw a distinction between circumstances where an acquisition would have the effect of substantially lessening competition, established on the balance of probabilities, and circumstances where an acquisition would be likely to have that effect, established on the basis that there is a real chance that that would be so, it can be seen that s 50(1) itself imposes its own differential standards of proof, at least so far as the determination of competitive effect is concerned. The utility of imposing differential standards, as a matter of legislative policy, is not at all clear, given that contravention will always be established on the lowest threshold being satisfied. Nevertheless, if one is to proceed on that basis in the present case, then one question involving one evaluative judgment emerges: would the acquisition be likely to substantially lessen competition in the relevant market?

> The answer to that question points to, and depends on, the interrelationship of all the facts, matters and circumstances which, in combination, define the future state of affairs that is characterised as being “likely”. If, for the purpose of satisfying the requisite legal standard, “likely” is taken to have the meaning of “a real chance”, then it is difficult to see why that standard should not apply to determine the existence and interrelationship of all those facts, matters and circumstances. If not, the possibility exists that different legal standards will intrude into inseparable elements of the calculus employed to detect change to the state of competition. As I have noted, a counterfactual is no more than an element of that calculus. Conceptually, it has no separate existence or purpose in the present context, other than as an aid to detect the existence and extent of change in the process of competition.

> Moreover, in the continuum of fact-finding, there may not be a bright line between those facts that determine the future state of a market and those facts that determine the future state of competition in that market. Indeed, one can envision examples where the facts that show the likely future state of the market will be the very facts that are determinative of a finding about the likely future state of competition in that market. In those cases, can fact-finding be regulated by two different standards of proof? To require, in those cases, the adoption, if that be conceptually possible, of a higher standard for one purpose (to determine the state of the market) would be to obliterate the threshold to which the second limb of s 50(1) has subjected the impugned conduct.

> (Original emphasis.)

[1273] The following points can be synthesised from AGL and Metcash.

[1274] First, the phrase “likely to have the effect” of a substantial lessening of competition requires only a real chance. There is no compelling reason why a test that is appropriate to determine the likely effect on competition in the context of s 45 should not also apply to s 50.

[1275] Second, the standard of a real chance implicit in the concept of likely is to be understood at a level which is commercially relevant and meaningful. I am concerned with real commercial likelihoods, not with mere possibilities, however plausible they might be.

[1276] Third, and importantly, the subject of the likelihood or real chance is singular in the sense that s 50 refers to the likely effect of substantially lessening competition and thus ultimately poses one question involving one evaluative judgment. In the present case, the application of s 50 turns on my satisfaction in relation to this single evaluative judgment, even though the exercise of determining whether the competitive effects of a transaction amount to a substantial lessening of competition involves multiple constituent inquiries, namely, identifying the futures with and without the transaction, identifying the effect on competition of each, and then making the relevant comparison leading to answering the one question that I have identified.

[1277] In this regard, it is a distraction to ask what standards of proof apply at each of the atomised constituent steps involved in assessing competitive effects. I am inclined to follow Yates J’s approach in Metcash. Yates J identified the potential problem with atomising s 50 in the following terms: “a counterfactual is no more than an element of [the] calculus … [I]t has no separate existence or purpose … other than as an aid to detect the existence and extent of change in the process of competition” (at [228]). That led his Honour to doubt the adoption of different legal standards for individual elements of the test because to do so could obliterate the statutory standard, which posed one question involving one evaluative judgment with that one evaluative judgment to be assessed on the basis of a real chance.

[1278] Now in this context, the ACCC is going too far in saying that it can necessarily establish a contravention of s 50 by proof of a real chance of the existence of a counterfactual and a real chance of a substantial lessening of competition in the sense that one real chance is compounded with another. Indeed, to apply s 50 on the basis of a real chance of a substantial lessening of competition built upon a real chance in the counterfactual may, depending upon how one does it, be erroneous. In doing so I would not have directed myself to the single statutory question and may have inappropriately reduced the probability inherent in the real chance to be assessed for the ultimate question.

[1279] Now I accept that the ACCC does not necessarily need to prove its counterfactual on the balance of probabilities. But the magnitude of any real chance that it demonstrates in respect of the alleged future states will practically and ultimately affect the magnitude of the real chance that it is able to demonstrate in respect of the alleged effects on competition and whether that rises to the requisite level of a likely effect of substantially lessening competition. Considering both the counterfactual and the alleged competitive effects together, the evaluation required compositely is whether a real commercial likelihood of a substantial lessening of competition has been shown.

#### (a) The ACCC’s case

[1280] Now as I have said, in order to assess whether the ART acquisition would be likely to have the effect of substantially lessening competition in a market, it is appropriate to compare the state of competition in the Interstate market(s) both “with” and “without” the ART acquisition, considered at the time at which the ART acquisition could be expected to be completed, that is, by the end of this month.

[1281] The ACCC submits that there is a real chance that PN’s acquisition of the ART would have the effect of substantially lessening competition with respect to the supply of the Rail Services to relevant users in one or more of the Interstate market(s).

[1282] In terms of the future with the ART acquisition, the ACCC submits the following.

[1283] The effect of the ART acquisition will be to substantially increase the barriers to entry for a party to commence supplying Rail Services in the Interstate market(s) and, as a consequence, it is unlikely that any party will consider commencing to supply Rail Services in the Interstate market(s). Mr James’ evidence is that Qube would not commence interstate intermodal operations if PN acquired the ART. The ACCC says that Qube’s position is indicative of the likely perspective of any other potential entrant.

[1284] As a result of the ART acquisition, so the ACCC says, PN will own and control the ART, including the BMUT, in circumstances where new entrants have no alternative terminal in the Brisbane area from which to provide Rail Services on interstate routes for at least the short to medium term.

[1285] The ACCC says that PN will have the ability and incentive, or be reasonably perceived to have the ability and incentive, to discriminate against new entrants in the Interstate market(s). As a result, in the future with the ART acquisition, there is a real chance that new entry will be significantly less likely to occur and PN will not be constrained by any potential or actual entry.

[1286] The ACCC says that the ART acquisition will confer control of the ART including the BMUT on PN, the dominant supplier of Rail Services on the North-South and East-West corridors. This raises barriers to entry relative to a future without the ART acquisition and those raised barriers bring about a lessening of competition in the relevant markets. The ACCC says that the significance of raising barriers to entry in this context is emphasised by the scale of PN’s operations, and the fact that it currently has only one competitor on the interstate routes. It is said that Qube’s evidence supports the ACCC’s case. But the ACCC says that Qube’s evidence is not the sum total of its case. The ACCC’s case is also about barriers to entry.

[1287] Further, the ACCC says that competition need not depend on whether someone will actually enter, but also (and more importantly) on whether the conditions are such that the threat of them entering is real enough to regulate competitive conduct.

[1288] The ACCC says that barriers to entry will be higher if the ART including the BMUT is in the hands of PN, compared to the hands of any other owner, whether Aurizon, Qube, Linfox, ARTC, or whichever other new entrant might be attracted by the prospect of lower barriers to entry and also wish to acquire the ART. The ACCC says that PN would face more constraints as a supplier of Rail Services if barriers were lower and the threat of entry was higher. It says that I do not need to be satisfied that Qube or any other particular entrant would definitely enter. It is enough that there is a real chance that any potential entrant would have a much better prospect of entering, such that PN would be more constrained by that prospect, in the future without the ART acquisition than in the future with the ART acquisition.

[1289] In relation to the future without the ART acquisition, the ACCC submits the following.

[1290] The ACCC says that the effect of the TSS should be disregarded for the purpose of the future without the ART acquisition analysis, on the basis that the making of the TSS contravened s 45 and therefore the TSS will be void and wholly or substantially unenforceable in the future without the ART acquisition. But I would say at this point that I disagree with the ACCC’s position on this.

[1291] First, I have found that the TSS does not infringe s 45.

[1292] Second, that being the case, the future without the ART acquisition does include the TSS. Now the ACCC says that the TSS would not have been executed without the proposed acquisition and that it was intended to operate interdependently with the ART BSA. But assuming that to be so, which is a contestable proposition as I have said earlier, takes the ACCC nowhere in terms of the s 50 case. That is because I am now looking forward for the purposes of s 50. And in the scenario today, and assuming a future without the ART acquisition, the TSS is now in place and operates.

[1293] Third, this is of course a problem for the ACCC because in the scenario without the ART acquisition, but with the TSS in place, Qube says that it will not enter. So Qube will not enter either:

  (a) in the future with the ART acquisition; or

  (b) in the future without the ART acquisition but with the TSS in place.

[1294] I will return to discuss the ramifications of this later. Let me return to how the ACCC is putting its case.

[1295] The ACCC says that in the future without the ART acquisition, it is likely that Aurizon would either retain ownership of the ART or continue the sale process for the ART with another non-PN entity acquiring the ART. I agree.

[1296] The ACCC says that in either case there is a real chance that:

  (a) the owner of the ART being either Aurizon or a third party would have the incentive to maximise utilisation of the ART with no countervailing consideration of that utilisation undermining revenue from interstate rail operations; and

  (b) barriers to entry would be low enough to enable new entry to occur.

[1297] In respect of barriers to entry, the ACCC has of course relied upon Dr Williams’ evidence that the presence of substantial barriers to entry in the relevant markets have important implications for assessing the effects of the impugned conduct on competition. In particular, if the impugned conduct further deters entry when some entry is possible, it may well be the case that the markets will need to wait a long time before another potential entrant is willing to accept the risks associated with entry.

[1298] So, Dr Williams observed that it is the height of the barriers that is critical for competition, as the barriers influence the possibility that market concentration could increase or decrease over time, regardless of whether or not one could identify a potential entrant.

[1299] Dr Williams went on to conclude:

> The ART Acquisition is likely to substantially lessen competition in the North-South Market. That market has been left highly concentrated with the exit of Aurizon and it has substantial barriers to entry. These barriers are likely to be raised further by the ability and incentive of Pacific National as the owner of the ART to discriminate against competitors in the North-South Market. The increase in barriers to entry to the North-South Market will have the likely effect of increasing concentration and increasing prices. These effects are not speculative because Qube has identified itself as a potential entrant that would be prevented from entering if Pacific National is the owner of the ART. My conclusion would be the same or stronger in the absence of the Undertaking, given that I have been instructed to assume that Pacific National would have the ability to discriminate against Rail Providers notwithstanding the Undertaking.

[1300] As I say, the ACCC says that if the ART acquisition proceeds, PN will gain control of the ART. This will raise barriers to entry for other providers of Rail Services into and out of Queensland. With that control of the ART, PN would have as owner of the ART the ability and incentive to deny access or provide access on discriminatory terms.

[1301] But the ACCC says that if the ART acquisition does not proceed, two things will happen. Either Aurizon will keep the ART or Aurizon will sell the ART to somebody else. Further, if PN does not control the ART including the BMUT, there will be:

  (a) increased competition to supply Rail Services; and

  (b) increased constraints on PN, creating increased competition in the supply of Rail Services.

[1302] The ACCC says that if the ART acquisition does not proceed, barriers to entry will be materially lower than if the ART acquisition proceeds, irrespective of whether Aurizon decides in future to sell the ART or the outcome of that sales process.

[1303] Let me turn now to discuss in more detail the ACCC’s case concerning the s 50(3) factors. As I have set out earlier, s 50(3) provides a non-exhaustive list of factors to be taken into account when considering the likely harm from the ART acquisition. Let me turn to the most relevant in the present context.

###### Effect of the ART acquisition on barriers to entry

[1304] The ACCC says that a viable interstate rail linehaul business requires terminal facilities proximate to Brisbane. I agree. The ACCC submits that the two known interstate terminal locations at SCT’s Bromelton terminal and the BMT at the Port of Brisbane are not likely to be suitable for any new entrant. Those terminals are either not available for access for a new entrant or are significantly inferior to the ART, leaving any new entrant materially competitively disadvantaged. I agree with those submissions. Further, the ACCC says that it is unlikely that a terminal will be built on a greenfield site, including the undeveloped land at ARTC’s Bromelton site, in the short to medium term over the next 3 to 5 years, and likely not for 7 to 10 years. I also agree with those submissions for the reasons that I have discussed elsewhere.

[1305] So the ACCC says that as a result of the ART acquisition, PN will own and control the ART including the BMUT in circumstances where there is no alternative terminal in the Brisbane area available to new entrants in the relevant timeframe. I agree.

[1306] Therefore, so the ACCC says, the critical issue facing any new entrant is seeking to obtain access to the BMUT on terms that new entrants consider reasonable to permit investment to establish a business as a rail operator on interstate routes. In this context, so the ACCC says, the potential for discriminatory conduct by PN in relation to access represents a strategic barrier to entry that is introduced by the ART acquisition. The ACCC says that the terms of the ART BSA do not impose any obligation on PN as to whether or on what terms it allows rail operators other than PN to use the ART or, specifically, the BMUT. Moreover, it says that PN will not be subject to any regulatory regime governing those matters.

[1307] Now I would say at this point that I agree with the ACCC so far as it goes and assuming that PN has not given a relevant undertaking. But the fact is that it has given a relevant undertaking or at least communicated its intention to do so. That substantially reduces the force of the ACCC’s concerns. Moreover and in any event, in reality the ACCC’s point is now reduced to focusing upon a hypothetical new entrant that it has not been able to identify. I say that because Qube will not even enter without the ART acquisition, because the TSS is now in place and I have rejected the ACCC’s s 45 case.

[1308] Let me return to the ACCC’s case.

[1309] As I have said, the ACCC says that comparing the futures with and without the ART acquisition reveals that the ART acquisition would be likely to raise barriers to entry, in that it would be likely to confer on PN an ability and incentive or perceived ability and incentive to discriminate against third party rail operators who sought to use the ART to supply North-South and East-West Rail Services. As a result, so the ACCC says, the ART acquisition has a real chance of deterring new entry, and therefore results in PN facing fewer competitive constraints in its supply of the relevant Rail Services to the relevant North-South and East-West users.

[1310] Now the respondents have submitted that the ACCC’s concerns with respect to a PN-owned ART being likely to deter potential new entry are misplaced and that Linfox’s acquisition of the QIB in conjunction with rail operations provided by Aurizon on the NCL supports their contention. The respondents implicitly equate Linfox’s position with that of a new entrant seeking access to the BMUT. But I agree with the ACCC that as PN does not operate its own rail services from the Queensland Terminal, the situation for Linfox is different. The QIB utilises the Queensland Terminal within the ART, but not the BMUT. And no other party uses the Queensland Terminal. PN utilises its own terminal at Tennyson. As such, PN has much less scope to discriminate against Linfox. It does not have to weigh Linfox’s interests against its own on a daily basis. But in another sense, the point can be flipped back the other way as I will explain later.

###### Concentration in the market caused by the ART acquisition

[1311] Now as the test for s 50 is forward looking from the likely transaction completion, the focus of the analysis must be on events from that time forward. But the ACCC says that whilst Aurizon closed its IIB in December 2017, being prior to the relevant time period in which the s 50 analysis is to be undertaken, the structure of the market(s) in mid 2019 is a direct consequence of that closure, which the ACCC submits was an inevitable consequence of the entry into of the various contractual arrangements by the respondents. Now that may be so, although I do not see where this really takes the ACCC given that I am to look forward and I do not consider the TSS to have contravened s 45.

[1312] Now it is the ACCC’s case that notwithstanding the current very high levels of concentration in the supply of Rail Services in the Interstate market(s):

  (a) the barriers to entry will significantly increase as a result of the ART acquisition; and

  (b) as a consequence, the current high level of concentration will be more likely to remain than might otherwise be the case should the ART acquisition not proceed.

[1313] As the ACCC points out, there are only two providers of Rail Services on the North-South and East-West corridors being PN and SCT. Dr Williams on certain assumptions calculated their respective shares of the supply of Rail Services (excluding bulk steel) on those routes as [Redacted]. Further, at least in respect of the North-South corridor, SCT’s offering is not as completely substitutable for that of PN.

[1314] The ACCC says that if the ART acquisition occurs, the real chance that any new entrant (including Qube) would commence supplying North-South and East-West Rail Services would be likely to disappear and, in the case of Qube, would clearly disappear, resulting in the maintenance of the current market dynamics and structure for the foreseeable future. That is to be contrasted with the competitive constraint that would otherwise be imposed on PN by virtue of the threat of or actual new entry.

###### Nature and extent of vertical integration caused by the ART acquisition

[1315] The ACCC says that in the future with the ART acquisition, PN will vertically integrate its rail operations on interstate routes with the operation of the ART. It says that this vertical integration will create a perception that PN’s incentives at the BMUT, together with its actual ability to give effect to those incentives, will ensure that its behaviour at the BMUT reflects the best interests of its own interstate rail operations, to the disadvantage of potential new entrants who require access to the BMUT in order to compete with PN’s interstate rail operations.

[1316] Contrastingly, if Aurizon retains ownership of the ART in the future without the ART acquisition, it would operate it as a non-vertically integrated owner and would have an incentive to promote new entry and greater utilisation of the ART. Alternatively, if a new sale process was held, then the acquirer could be a non-vertically integrated infrastructure owner or a new rail linehaul player. In the case of the former, its incentives would be the same as Aurizon, and it would likely use its control over the ART to promote new entry into the Interstate market(s). In the case of the latter, the ACCC says that no evidence has been adduced to establish that an alternative acquirer of the ART would immediately (or even in the short term) commence supplying services in the Interstate market(s) upon completion of the acquisition. Accordingly, an alternative acquirer of the ART, being potentially a rail linehaul player, would have no incentive to discriminate against PN in at least the short to medium term, as the revenue from PN’s volume as the major user of the BMUT would be the main source of revenue for the acquirer. But there is a real chance, however, and so the ACCC says, that an alternative acquirer may at some stage in the future seek to commence supplying interstate Rail Services. Acquiring the ART would lower the alternate ART owner’s barriers to entry into the Interstate market(s). This lowering of barriers to entry would increase competitive constraints above those that would otherwise apply to PN in the future with the ART acquisition.

[1317] Now it is convenient at this point to digress slightly and to say something further concerning vertical integration and the question of efficiencies.

[1318] Mr Smith gave evidence that the ART acquisition will give rise to efficiencies resulting from vertical integration, emphasising in particular efficiencies resulting from the elimination of double marginalisation. But I agree with the ACCC that efficiencies should not directly impact on my assessment of whether the ART acquisition is likely to substantially lessen competition, and should not be directly taken into account in applying s 50 to the ART acquisition.

[1319] It may be accepted that the outcome of competition may be to produce or conducive to producing productive, allocative and dynamic efficiencies. But to talk of the favourable benefits of competition is not the same thing as talking about competition itself and whether it will be enhanced by vertical integration or detracted thereby. To accept for the sake of the argument that vertical integration may produce allocative efficiency does not deny that the effect of the vertical integration may be to produce a lesser state of competition than the scenario where there is no vertical integration.

[1320] But I agree with the ACCC that in any event there are difficulties with Mr Smith’s views. Whilst Dr Williams and Mr Smith both agree that vertical mergers may give rise to efficiencies from the elimination of double marginalisation, Dr Williams disagrees with Mr Smith that the ART acquisition by PN will result in these types of efficiencies. And the ACCC says that Mr Smith’s contention that the ART acquisition would eliminate inefficiencies resulting from double marginalisation should be rejected. Before proceeding further, let me re-iterate the theory that I have described earlier.

[1321] As Mr Smith explained, the concept of internalisation of “double mark-ups”, often referred to as double marginalisation, is one of the sources of pro-competitive efficiencies that may arise in the context of a non-horizontal merger. Often this drives the incentive to create a lower-priced bundle. As he said, this result can be easiest to describe in terms of vertically related entities, although the underlying driver of this result is an economic complementarity of whatever source between the merging parties’ offerings.

[1322] As he explained, pre-merger an upstream firm will set a price to maximise profits so that any further decrease in price, which would reduce its margins, and hence profitability per unit, would be balanced by an equal gain in profits that would be generated by an increase in sales volumes. When that upstream firm sells an intermediate good to a downstream firm, the downstream firm takes into account the upstream price when setting downstream prices. Downstream prices are then also independently set so as to maximise profits, again so that any further decrease in price, which would reduce the downstream firm’s margins, and hence profitability per unit, would be balanced by an equal gain in profits that would be generated by an increase in sales volumes.

[1323] But as he explained, post-merger the upstream entity may now consider reducing its prices. As in the pre-merger scenario, this will reduce the upstream entity’s margin, but will generate an off-setting gain in profits through an increase in the volume of goods sold by the upstream entity. However, the upstream entity can now also consider the impact on the downstream entity that is now a related entity. Not only will that downstream entity benefit from the lower cost of intermediate products, but it will typically pass on some of those lower costs to its own customers, resulting in an expansion of its volumes of sales, and profitability. Although pre-merger, the upstream and the downstream firms are under separate ownership, and therefore the beneficial impact of a price decrease on each other’s sales is not taken into account, post-merger such mutually beneficial effects would be considered when setting upstream and downstream prices, possibly resulting in lower prices.

[1324] As Massimo Motta explained it (Motta M, Competition Policy: Theory and Practice (Cambridge University Press, 2004) at 307 to 308):

> …Suppose that a manufacturer relies on a retailer for selling to final customers, and that the former sells to the latter according to a constant unit price (linear pricing). Suppose also, for simplicity, that the retailer does not have any costs other than the wholesale price…

> Both firms want to maximise their profit and in order to do so they both choose the monopolistic mark-up (margin) over their own cost: the upstream firm chooses the wholesale price w given its cost c, and the downstream firm as a result will choose p given its own cost, namely the wholesale price w (which is higher than c because the upstream firm has a margin). The result, however, of both firms adding their margin is that they end up with customers paying too high a price (buying too few units) with respect to what would be optimal from their joint point of view, that is from the point of view of the vertical chain (the sum of the profits made by the upstream and downstream firm).

> If instead the two firms are under the same management, the final price p would be chosen so as to add only a mark-up over the cost c. Vertical integration – that is the merger of two firms – is efficient since it allows them to coordinate on the optimal outcome, or to “internalise” (control for) the externality that they impose on each other. The result after the correction for this externality is that not only firms but also consumers gain from the vertical merger…

[1325] Now the ACCC’s arguments on this question are the following.

[1326] First, the ACCC says that the elimination of double marginalisation could also be achieved in a future without the ART acquisition if an alternative acquirer were also to commence supplying Rail Services in the Interstate market(s). But I would note that clearly this would not be to the same extent. As the ACCC contended in other parts of its case, a new entrant including Qube would be starting from much lower volumes.

[1327] Second, the ACCC says that it is not necessary for firms to vertically integrate in order to eliminate double marginalisation. A non-integrated upstream owner of the ART could also eliminate double marginalisation by adopting “non-linear” access charges that involve both a fixed component and a variable component per unit. There is nothing to prevent a non-integrated acquirer of the ART in a future without the ART acquisition from adopting this charging structure. Indeed, Aurizon currently charges PN both fixed and variable components in its charges for access to the ART. And the ACCC says that Mr Smith has not done any analysis to determine whether in a future without the ART acquisition an alternative acquirer of the ART would or would not set non-linear prices in a way that eliminates double marginalisation. I agree with the ACCC’s points.

[1328] Third, the ACCC says that Mr Smith’s theoretical arguments have not been supported with evidence quantifying the reduction to PN’s rail linehaul prices that would result from the elimination of double marginalisation, or detailed lay evidence referring to the elimination of double marginalisation as a benefit of the ART acquisition. As Dr Williams questioned, “…is the real driver for the vertical merger the avoidness of double marginalisation or not? That’s really the issue. And … until this issue was raised by Mr Smith, it never occurred to me that double marginalisation would be driving this acquisition”. I tend to agree. Now to be fair, some PN documents do implicitly touch on such a benefit, but it could hardly be said that the elimination of double marginalisation was a principal driver of PN’s strategy.

[1329] Fourth, the ACCC says that a vertically integrated downstream firm retains the discretion as to whether they pass on their cost reductions to their customers. In the present case the prices for Rail Services can be negotiated. Accordingly, the downstream firm’s decision of whether a cost reduction will be passed on to customers may depend on the attractiveness of the customers’ other options. One cannot be confident that PN will pass on such cost reductions to their customers who have less compelling outside options. I agree.

[1330] Fifth, I should also note that Dr Williams gave some other evidence on this topic to the following effect:

> So the theory is right, of course. It’s very well acknowledged. But the question as to its relevance here, it seems to me – and you can see the difference in the joint report – is that, in my opinion, it is of very little relevance in this particular case because it’s contingent upon there being a margin on marginal cost that the – sorry, that the price – the unit price that the downstream firm pays for the – access to the upstream input, if there’s no vertical integration, includes a price – a margin on the marginal cost; that – that is, to translate it into language of the people who are represented in this room, it assumes that at the moment Pacific National is paying a margin on marginal cost for every unit of access that it gets to the ART.

> And as I understand, the charges that Pacific National is paying for access to the ART are principally fixed with respect to the amount of usage that it makes of the ART. So it won’t enter into the derivative of its costs with respect to quantity. That is, they’re fixed with respect to quantity, that the – its usage rather than variable. So that’s why I say in response to proposition 22 in the joint report that I agree with the proposition that double marginalisation is unlikely to cause inefficiency if without vertical integration the revenue accruing to the owner of the ART is predominantly fixed with respect to the use of input. What I mean by fixed is fixed with respect to changes in quantity; that is, whether it – whether it affects the derivative of total cost with respect to quantity.

[1331] But Mr Smith disputed this aspect in the following terms:

> …Having regard to marginisation, I again refer to the factual evidence. I thought that there is a fixed component, and a substantial variable component, and, moreover, Pacific National’s position is that on – on acquisition – obviously TSAs fall away. If you own the terminal you don’t have a TSA, so you don’t have that fixed nor variable component. So the variable component, necessarily, is a marginal cost. It comes into your accounting, and that’s quite substantial. I won’t go to the confidential numbers. Moreover, if you don’t have to bear that fixed component each year, it doesn’t have to get into your calculus ever. Now, the economic saying is that all costs are variably in the long run, but if there is an annually recurring charge, you know you are going to have to cover that. On your north-south route you’re going to have to price that in at some point over the year. If you suddenly lose that obligation that should effect your pricing incentives, given that many of these pricing incentives are not day to day, they are year on year contracts.

> And, finally, PN claims that it will substantially reduce the costs. So, obviously, it will not pay anyone. It will take those costs inside, but those will be far lower than it pays at the moment, and substantially fixed. So I agree there will be a move – I think there’s a move from a part-variable part-fixed component to a much lower recurring fixed charge each year.

[1332] I do not need to resolve this point, but if I need to say so I found Dr Williams’ position to be more compelling.

[1333] In summary, the ACCC submits that it is unlikely that the ART acquisition will lead to the elimination of double marginalisation and give rise to corresponding efficiencies, and in any case this is not directly relevant to a substantial lessening of competition analysis under s 50. I agree with the ACCC’s submissions if only on the direct relevance question. Even if PN could establish relevant efficiencies from the elimination of double marginalisation, which in my view it has not done so in clear terms, to show that benefit would not show that there was not a substantial lessening of competition. Productive, allocative and dynamic efficiencies may be the fruits of competition, but producing allocative efficiency by the elimination of double marginalisation does not disprove a substantial lessening of competition flowing from the vertical integration even with the associated benefit flowing from the said elimination if it could be quantified and substantiated at a meaningful level.

###### Absence of countervailing power relevant to the ART acquisition

[1334] The ACCC says that customers do not possess significant countervailing power to defeat price rises that affect relevant users. The ACCC referred to the following matters as examples.

[1335] [Redacted]

[1336] [Redacted]

[1337] [Redacted]

[1338] I agree with the ACCC that each of these examples are inconsistent with the proposition that these customers possess any material degree of countervailing power sufficient to defeat a SSNIP imposed by PN. But in my view this may be the case in either relevant futures.

###### Likelihood of PN significantly and sustainably increasing prices or profit margins due to the ART acquisition

[1339] The ACCC submits that there is a group of customers for Rail Services for whom, in the event of a SSNIP, alternative linehaul services do not exist, either for the whole of their freight task or for a substantial part of their freight task. For these relevant users, PN will be able to grant lesser discounts (or withhold discounts altogether) and/or increase prices for the Rail Services as explained elsewhere. I agree with these submissions, although it may be said that this may be the case in either relevant futures.

###### Extent to which substitutes are available

[1340] The ACCC says that the evidence demonstrates that relevant users have no realistic economic substitutes. I agree with these submissions and have discussed that matter elsewhere.

[1341] The ACCC says that the lack of alternatives for relevant users will enable PN to:

  (a) increase the price it charges for the linehaul services provided to the relevant users whether directly or indirectly;

  (b) decrease the level of discounts offered to relevant users; or

  (c) continue to charge prices which it would not be able to if faced with actual, or the threat of, entry by a new supplier of rail linehaul services.

[1342] And it says that this impact will be exacerbated in the case of those relevant users for whom delivery to any location other than the BMUT would involve significant additional cost or relevant users for whom SCT is not capable of meeting their needs.

[1343] Now I agree with the ACCC concerning a lack of substitutes for relevant users. But in my view, if PN is allowed to proceed with the ART acquisition with an appropriate undertaking, PN’s powers in the respects just contended for by the ACCC will not be significantly enhanced, if at all, from the future without the ART acquisition.

###### Other s 50(3) factors

[1344] I have not directly addressed the s 50(3)(a), (g) and (h) factors. But let me say the following. First, the actual and potential level of import competition is not directly relevant given the type of Rail Services I am considering. Second, no party directly addressed s 50(3)(g) being any relevant and substantial dynamic characteristics of the relevant markets. I have, of course, discussed elsewhere the possibility of new terminals in the relevant timeframe and service differentiation, but under other topics. Third, s 50(3)(h) is not directly relevant. The IIB has closed down and the QIB has been sold to Linfox. And the ART acquisition will not result in the removal of a competitor. Rather, the question has been whether it may deter new entry.

###### Conclusion as to effect of the ART acquisition

[1345] In summary, the ACCC says that comparing the future state of competition with and without the ART acquisition demonstrates that the likely effect of the ART acquisition is that:

  (a) PN would have the ability and incentive, or perceived ability and incentive, to discriminate against third parties in favour of its own operations with respect to access to the BMUT;

  (b) it would materially raise barriers to entry for the supply of North-South and/or East-West Rail Services for any party contemplating entry into the market(s) for the provision of these services; and

  (c) as a result, it would be unlikely that Qube or any other party would commence supplying North-South or East-West Rail Services.

[1346] It says that these factors would combine to materially reduce the competitive constraint on PN in the Interstate market(s) for the foreseeable future.

[1347] Further, the ACCC says that PN would also face weak competitive constraint in relation to the supply of Rail Services to relevant users on either the North-South or East-West corridors (or both) from SCT or from road services or sea services. As a result, the relevant users would face little alternative but to continue to acquire those services from PN if PN increased the prices (or reduced the discounts) for North-South or East-West Rail Services, or decreased the quality of those services.

[1348] Accordingly, so the ACCC says, there is a real chance that the ART acquisition will substantially lessen competition with respect to the supply of Rail Services to relevant users in one or more of the Interstate market(s). So, the ART acquisition would be likely to have the effect of substantially lessening competition in the Interstate market(s) and would contravene s 50(1).

#### (b) Analysis

[1349] Before addressing at length some of the key issues, let me make the following points.

[1350] First, concentration in the relevant markets is not likely to be higher in the counterfactual than in the factual.

[1351] Second, although if PN were to own the ART it would have an ability and incentive to discriminate against any access seekers, this can be dealt with by a relevant undertaking that I will discuss in the next section. But query who those access seekers would be. Qube will not enter in the factual or counterfactual including the TSS. And no one else has been identified.

[1352] Third, and related to the second point, if PN were to own the ART, access seekers would not have a reasonable perception that PN would have the ability and incentive to discriminate against them providing that a relevant undertaking was given.

[1353] Fourth, I accept that barriers to entry in the absence of an undertaking may be heightened, but that may not be sufficient of itself to make out a s 50 case. Moreover, the undertaking ameliorates or gets rid of that concern.

[1354] Let me turn to some specific matters.

###### Market concentration

[1355] Now in the future without the ART acquisition but with the TSS, Qube would not start to supply interstate intermodal services. And in the future with the ART acquisition, Qube would not also start.

[1356] Further, even if Qube acquired the ART without an intermodal business, the prospect of Qube commencing supply of interstate intermodal services would be quite unclear. Even with the benefit of customer contracts from the QIB, Qube estimated that it would take at least 3 to 5 years to commence supplying interstate intermodal services. But without those contracts it would take even longer. At best, 3 to 5 years could only be a “target”. But beyond that timeframe other alternative terminals to the ART may come on stream.

[1357] Now the ACCC says that:

  (a) in the future with the ART acquisition, PN would be the larger of the two remaining suppliers of intermodal rail services in the Interstate markets;

  (b) in the future without the ART acquisition:

    (i) someone would have acquired the ART and commenced supplying Rail Services in the Interstate markets; or

    (ii) Aurizon would have retained or someone other than PN would have acquired the ART and not commenced supplying such Rail Services, but this would in any event have reduced or not increased barriers to entry.

[1358] But Qube is the only entity that the ACCC has identified as possibly acquiring the ART and commencing supply of Rail Services in the Interstate markets.

[1359] Accordingly, in substance, the ACCC’s case in respect of market concentration concerning Rail Services in the Interstate markets is that:

  (a) in the future with the ART acquisition, PN and SCT would supply services in the Interstate markets; and

  (b) in the future without the ART acquisition, Aurizon would have continued with its sale process for the ART, and Qube would have acquired the ART and commenced supplying intermodal rail services in the Interstate markets.

[1360] So, in terms of the market concentration question, the ACCC’s alleged future without the ART acquisition depends substantially on evidence from Qube. But I have viewed Qube’s position and evidence in relation to the litigation with some caution. Qube has been a driving force behind the development of the ACCC’s case theory and the commencement of this proceeding. It is not a disinterested entity. It is a commercial actor with commercial interests opposed to PN’s. And it is not unfair to say that Qube tailored its interactions with the ACCC strategically, in order to steer the regulatory process in directions favourable to it.

[1361] But in any event I do not accept the ACCC’s alleged future without the ART acquisition. Qube would not enter even in that scenario because of the existence of the TSS.

[1362] Now for completeness I should say that PN says that as to acquiring the ART in the future without the ART acquisition, Qube is not likely to acquire the ART because Qube is not interested in acquiring the ART without the QIB. But I cannot rule out the possibility that Qube may have still wished to acquire the ART without also securing an intermodal business, but only in the absence of the TSS. Qube may want the ART as an income producing asset without any intention to enter the intermodal business in the next 3 to 5 years due to the unavailability of a business of that character, although query whether such a desire in and of itself properly raises competition concerns.

[1363] But if Qube were to acquire the ART and consider commencing supply of interstate intermodal services without having first acquired an intermodal business, the prospect of it commencing supply of those services and when would be speculative to some extent. Qube estimated that even with the benefit of customer contracts from the QIB, it would take at least 3 to 5 years to commence supplying interstate intermodal services. Without those contracts it would take longer, with 3 to 5 years simply a target. Mr James’ affidavit evidence was to the effect that whether entry occurs at all appears to depend on whether Qube can somehow find additional volumes, acquire a freight forwarding business or enter into a joint venture with Linfox.

[1364] Further, a new terminal is likely to be built as a result of the Inland Rail Project in no later than 10 years. ARTC has already acquired land at Bromelton to build such a terminal. As Mr Keyte explained, new terminal(s) will be built during the course of construction of the project, and ARTC’s proposed terminal at Bromelton may be completed in as little as 3 to 5 years. So on any view there is a limited window in which any lessening of competition could occur.

###### Barriers to entry

[1365] The ACCC says that the acquisition by PN of the ART would raise barriers to entry in the Interstate market(s) because PN would have the ability and incentive to discriminate against a potential provider of Rail Services that seeks access to the ART or PN would be perceived as having such an ability and incentive.

[1366] Now PN says that it would not have the ability to discriminate in the future with the ART acquisition for two reasons.

[1367] First, discrimination would likely contravene s 46 and, to be effective, would be required to occur on a scale that would easily be detectable and would require the knowledge and assistance of a range of PN employees.

[1368] Second, PN has offered an undertaking to the Court that would preclude it from engaging in discriminatory conduct, which undertaking contains a range of measures that would likely ensure that any discrimination could be detected, and that would provide any reasonable access seeker with confidence that it would not face any discrimination.

[1369] Now as I have already said I reject the first point and accept the second point.

[1370] Further, PN says that it would not have any incentive to discriminate for the following reasons.

[1371] First, discrimination of the kind that the ACCC hypothesises would cause damage to PN’s own rail business. It would cause delays to PN’s trains, create safety issues and lead to backed up trucks, inconvenience to its customers and reputational harm.

[1372] Second, to be effective, the discrimination would need to occur regularly, over an extended period of time and on a scale that materially and consistently damages the access seeker’s business. That could not occur without detection and nor could it occur without the knowledge and assistance of a range of PN employees. PN says that the necessary premises for such discrimination depart from reality.

[1373] Third, Mr Smith undertook an analysis and estimated that [Redacted] of a new entrant’s volumes would need to be taken from PN before it might be worthwhile for PN to seek to defend against that loss of volume by forgoing terminal profits. But PN says that it is unlikely that that would be the case. It says that Aurizon and PN were not close competitors. And even if Qube were to enter the Interstate markets, it would be a more distant competitor than Aurizon. Further, no other potential entrant has been identified by the ACCC.

[1374] Fourth, PN would be cognisant of the likelihood that if it were to engage in discrimination of the kind the ACCC hypothesises, access seekers could build a new terminal or promote such a development.

[1375] Now there is some force in PN’s points, but in my view it is likely that PN would have an incentive to discriminate. But this can be adequately addressed by the undertaking that I will require as I will discuss in the final section of my reasons.

[1376] Further, and with the appropriate undertaking, the evidence does not support a finding that access seekers would reasonably perceive that PN would have an ability and incentive to discriminate. In terms of perception, let me elaborate further.

[1377] First, Qube’s asserted views were exaggerated, and I suspect for their own strategic purposes so that they could acquire the ART cheaply. Qube witnesses said that they would perceive PN to have an ability and incentive to discriminate regardless of any undertaking in place, that is, even if discrimination was objectively wholly unlikely in any meaningful and sustained sense because of the undertaking. I consider that Qube’s perceptions were overstated and not reflective of a reasonable potential new entrant in the future world with both the ART acquisition and the undertaking.

[1378] Second, Linfox gave evidence that it did not have any such concern when it acquired the QIB, even though it would need to access the ART and PN had agreed to acquire the terminal. I agree with Aurizon that Linfox’s purchase of the QIB provides some test for the ACCC’s theory. Mr Ian Strachan, the President of Intermodal for Linfox, said:

> Based on my experience of Pacific National’s operation of rail terminals across Australia I do not believe that Linfox’s operation of the QIB is likely to be adversely affected if Pacific National acquires the Acacia Ridge Terminal and becomes the counterparty to the Linfox Acacia Ridge Arrangements…

[1379] There is little basis to not accept the evidence of Mr Strachan, and no challenge was made.

[1380] Further, and contrary to the ACCC’s position seeking to distinguish Linfox’s views, the fact that PN does not use the Queensland Terminal may make it easier, not harder, for PN to offer Linfox poor quality service at the Queensland Terminal. Unlike the BMUT, there is no risk that any delays by PN will have an adverse impact on PN’s own services.

[1381] Third, the ACCC has not called SCT or any other entity whose perception might be relevant.

[1382] Fourth and more generally, it is untenable to take an erroneous perception of an ability and incentive to discriminate into account in determining whether conduct is likely to substantially lessen competition.

[1383] As I have said and again to accept PN’s submissions on this aspect, the purpose of the CCA is to increase the welfare of Australians through the promotion of competition. Competition enhances welfare because its consequence is economic efficiency, whether productive, allocative or innovative efficiency. Taking an erroneous perception into account in assessing whether conduct is likely to substantially lessen competition would not increase economic efficiency. To the contrary, it would prevent assets from being acquired by the entities that can operate them most efficiently and therefore place the most value on them. It would accordingly decrease economic efficiency. Moreover, if potential investors know that the price they will obtain from the future sale of assets may be reduced by potential bidders’ erroneous perceptions, it would likely decrease the amount that they were willing to pay for those assets. It would therefore prevent capital flows from being directed to the places where they will be most effective, resulting in a reduction in efficiency as well.

[1384] Moreover, at least for any erroneous perception to be relevant it must be reasonable. But in the present context, Qube’s perceptions of the world with both the ART acquisition and the undertaking are not reasonable. Qube has had access to commercial instruments that it would not normally see in the ordinary course. And it has received sophisticated legal advice from expert competition lawyers viewing those instruments through the prism of competition law, and seeking to advance Qube’s strategic objectives. But perceptions driven by such factors are likely to be artificial. Qube’s views in my opinion are not a reliable indicator of the views any other potential entrant might have in the world with both the ART acquisition and the undertaking.

###### Other relevant constraints

[1385] Further, other constraints could ameliorate any substantial lessening of competition if the ART acquisition went ahead, although not sufficiently in my view in the absence of an undertaking.

[1386] First, PN would be constrained by the potential for access seekers to build a new terminal like SCT has done in Bromelton and PN itself considered doing at the Port of Brisbane.

[1387] Second, the Inland Rail Project will likely be completed in no later than 10 years. Other terminals will be built during the course of construction of that project. ARTC has already acquired land to construct a terminal at Bromelton site. That new terminal in Bromelton may be completed within 5 years.

[1388] I agree with the respondents that there is no realistic possibility that any lessening of competition could continue after the completion of the Inland Rail Project. And before then, the only potential entrant that the ACCC can identify is Qube. But as the respondents correctly contend, that is unsurprising given that barriers to entry are high in any event. But even if Qube were to enter, it would at best be targeting entry in 3 to 5 years. More generally, whether entry would occur in the window in which any lessening of competition could realistically occur is to some extent speculative.

[1389] Let me further elaborate on these matters as they are not unimportant.

[1390] In relation to the Inland Rail Project and the development of intermodal terminals in light of that project the evidence was as follows.

[1391] On 24 November 2016, ARTC purchased 850 hectares of land at Bromelton. A press release of ARTC at the time stated the following:

> Given the site’s strategic location and the intended scope for the area to see an intermodal logistics hub or ‘inland port’ to be developed over time – it was important we looked at the opportunity closely when the land became available.

> The land is the only area on the eastern seaboard with current direct access to the interstate railway line between Melbourne and Brisbane. The site is also adjacent to B-double transport routes that are less than one hour’s drive from the Port of Brisbane and the Brisbane CBD.

> …

> There are no immediate plans by ARTC to develop the Bromelton site, but early planning for that process will start over coming months.

[1392] In its “indicative proposal” for the purchase of the ART, ARTC stated:

> Acquiring Acacia Ridge allows ARTC to commence development of its Bromelton site at the optimum time so that in the long term, migration of the interstate freight to Bromelton is made possible.

[1393] On 24 July 2017, the Port of Brisbane made a submission to an inquiry into National Freight Supply Chain priorities. As part of that submission, Port of Brisbane referred to an expectation that there would be “new, more remote intermodal terminals in South East Queensland (e.g. at Bromelton, Ebenezer and Toowoomba)”.

[1394] On 14 May 2018, a letter from the federal Minister for Infrastructure and Transport and the Minister for Finance stated:

> We also share your enthusiasm for the Inland Rail Project and are delighted to be partnering with the ARTC on delivering this long-term economic investment for Australia, and wish to raise several matters regarding this project.

The letter went on to recognise the importance of intermodal terminals to the success of inland rail and to refer to various details of the development of the Inland Rail Project, including a discrete body of work with which the ARTC would be involved, to inform the government’s consideration of intermodal terminals in Melbourne and Brisbane.

[1395] A strategy paper presented to the Port of Brisbane in August 2018 referred to the Inland Rail Project being a “catalyst for intermodal terminal developments throughout the east coast of Australia” and went on to say:

> A key part of the scope of the MBIR business case assumes intermodal terminals in Melbourne and Brisbane as well as regional terminals would be established along the corridor via private investment.

That paper went on to refer inter-alia to various potential terminals in Toowoomba, Bromelton and Ebeneezer.

[1396] Mr Keyte, Chief Operating Officer of the Port of Brisbane, also gave evidence about the matter. Mr Keyte summarised the Port of Brisbane’s expectations in respect of the Inland Rail Project as follows:

> HIS HONOUR: I suppose my assumption was that ARTC wouldn’t develop any terminal at Bromelton until such time as the inland rail terminal had been completed. Is that a fair assumption or not?---Your Honour, I think – I think it’s fair, but I would use the term that inland rail was assured to be completed.

> Yes?---I would – would assume that they – if they knew for sure that inland rail was under construction, then terminals would also be constructed to be open at the same time.

> All right. I’m just trying to work out whether it’s a realistic possibility that somebody would look at the Acacia Ridge terminal to be used first before, say, another terminal had been developed at Bromelton, or to use whatever is coming through from the inland rail?---Yes, your Honour. So – so inland rail is – is gazetted to be completed at Acacia Ridge, which means that in our context Acacia Ridge is the rail terminal for inland rail.

> Yes?---And we believe inland rail is some 10 years away, but we don’t think Acacia Ridge has a life expectancy beyond that 10 years. As I said, I think other terminals will evolve during the construction phases of inland rail.

[1397] Let me say something further about Bromelton.

[1398] At the time of considering the ART transaction, PN was also comparing the ART to the possibility of a new terminal at Bromelton.

[1399] Further, there was evidence that Qube had also potentially planned for an intermodal terminal at Bromelton in the future. In March 2017 the Qube board approved a five year business plan. Part of that plan involved Qube creating or facilitating “Moorebank-type developments in Melbourne and Brisbane”. Mr James accepted that this was a reference to Qube developing an intermodal hub at Bromelton either at the ARTC site at Bromelton or elsewhere. But Mr James said that the plan was not to complete the terminal in 5 years, but rather that he would like to think that something would be on the ground in 5 years. In May 2017, Mr James referred to his expectation of an intermodal hub being built at Bromelton, and that the Inland Rail Project would see large intermodal terminals built at Melbourne and Brisbane to provide greater efficiency for local logistics, and that such terminals could be like the Moorebank terminal.

[1400] The evidence before me demonstrates not only that the Inland Rail Project is being developed, but that it is likely to lead to the development of new intermodal terminals, particularly in the next 5 to 10 years.

[1401] Further, the most recent example of a new entrant, SCT, secured the development of its service with a new terminal at Bromelton. It started its own terminal when apparently it was not able to get access to the ART.

[1402] Now even assuming that entry by Qube was likely, in my view any such entry is likely to be no earlier than the next 5 years. And it may be that an alternative terminal would be constructed within as early as 5 years.

[1403] Further, in the world with the ART acquisition, PN having made a significant investment in the ART may want to ensure that it remains as the main terminal in Brisbane and surrounds. Such a position may be threatened if any new entrant became disenchanted with the availability of access at the ART, and instead sponsored the creation of an alternative terminal at another location such as Bromelton. Such a perception may discipline its behaviour against discrimination, although not completely in the absence of an undertaking. Further, this risk is well apparent in the period prior to the commencement of the Inland Rail Project. Any support by another entity or entities for a new terminal elsewhere, or reports of difficulties obtaining proper access at the ART, may lead to government support for an alternative terminal.

[1404] In summary, in my view the conduct of PN will be to some extent constrained by the possibility of new terminals although not sufficiently in my view in the absence of an undertaking.

[1405] Further, the other competitive constraint on PN is SCT although I would not wish to overstate the level of such a constraint.

[1406] SCT previously carried out intermodal operations by way of a “hook and pull” arrangement with Aurizon. After that arrangement ceased in January 2016, SCT constructed its own intermodal terminal at Bromelton, near Brisbane. It built that terminal at a cost of approximately $35 million but including approximately $10 million from the Commonwealth Government and commenced operations in January 2017.

[1407] SCT is a competitor to PN. Following its entry to the market, SCT won significant volumes of work. When Aurizon announced its decision to exit the interstate market, Woolworths moved its Melbourne-Brisbane rail business to SCT and not to PN or any freight forwarder using PN. More generally, after Aurizon’s exit from the interstate market in 2017, some customers went to SCT despite SCT being a smaller operator than PN.

[1408] The position of SCT is not altered by the ART transaction. It remains a competitive constraint on PN’s intermodal business to some significant extent.

###### Conclusion

[1409] Let me make some final points.

[1410] First, even if Qube were to own the ART and were to commence supply of interstate intermodal rail services, and commence supply of interstate intermodal services within the limited window in which the ACCC contends a lessening of competition could occur, Qube’s entry may not create conditions any more competitive than a future with the ART acquisition.

[1411] Qube has not offered to give an undertaking under s 87B or otherwise to grant access to the ART to third parties. It could therefore refuse to grant access to the terminal altogether, including to PN once PN’s current TSA expires or grant access on unfavourable terms.

[1412] Further, on the ACCC’s own case, as soon as Qube achieves significant market share in the North-South Rail Services market, it would have the same ability and incentive to discriminate that the ACCC alleges that PN has to discriminate. Indeed, even before Qube’s market share increased to the level required to discriminate profitably, Qube may have an incentive to discriminate against PN or any access seeker. That is because even if the discrimination is not immediately profitable, it would disadvantage PN or the access seeker and thereby increase Qube’s market share allowing Qube to achieve required scale more quickly.

[1413] Second, in the future without the ART acquisition but with the TSS in place, Aurizon would still control PN’s behaviour and could prevent discrimination. But in the future with the ART acquisition, Aurizon of course would not act as a discipline on PN’s behaviour. But in that scenario, that is, in the future with the ART acquisition, that scenario also includes the discipline imposed by the undertaking, which I will discuss in the next section. And indeed there is even greater advantage in that scenario as the undertaking deals expressly with provisions concerning, inter-alia, capacity enhancements, which is not part of the scenario in the future without the ART acquisition.

[1414] Third, in the world without the ART acquisition and assuming that Aurizon continued to own the ART, it would have:

  (a) no obligation to provide non-discriminatory access; and

  (b) no obligation to capacity expand or participate in a process directed to achieving such a result.

[1415] But in the world with both the ART acquisition and the undertaking, PN would have such obligations. So in the factual as opposed to the counterfactual, potential new entrants are better protected.

[1416] Indeed the difference between the two cases is also further heightened if in the counterfactual (i.e. no ART acquisition) Qube were to acquire the ART from Aurizon. Qube suggests that such an acquisition by it may be a precursor to entry into of the relevant markets. But in that scenario it would have no obligation to provide non-discriminatory access and no obligation potentially to capacity expand, unlike PN in the future with both the ART acquisition and the undertaking.

[1417] Fourth, in the future without the ART acquisition, the TSS remains in place and as I have said does not give rise to any s 45 question. But in such a scenario, that is, where the TSS is in place, Qube has in my view definitively said that it will not enter. But what then is the ACCC’s case?

[1418] The ACCC says that because Qube at least would not enter in that scenario, the substantial lessening of competition arises due to increased barriers to entry only. It is said that if I find that Qube’s reason for not entering is irrational and idiosyncratic, it follows that there remains the potential for a rationally acting new entrant to enter the Interstate market(s). But in some respects this is speculation. No one else has been realistically identified by the ACCC although I invited the ACCC to identify others. Moreover, the question of barriers to entry is not to be assessed in a vacuum. The question is whether realistically there are other potential entrants who may or would be likely to enter in the relevant timeframe absent the barrier or increased barrier to entry created by the acquisition. Raising barriers to entry does not necessarily establish a s 50 case. Having said that, I am inclined to accept Dr Williams’ evidence that raising barriers will more likely preserve the current high market concentration and act less as a discipline on PN’s behaviour because the threat of new entry is less. But that is all in the absence of an undertaking. With an undertaking and the ART acquisition I do not consider that there is likely to be any substantial lessening of competition comparing the likely relevant futures.

[1419] Fifth, for completeness let me deal with another hypothetical. On my reasoning I have said that in the world without the ART acquisition the TSS will be in place. But let me assume that the TSS would not be in place because, contrary to my findings, it would contravene s 45.

[1420] In that scenario it is a realistic possibility that Qube could acquire the ART.

[1421] But in the future without the ART acquisition and assuming that Qube were to purchase the ART, Qube would then be vertically integrated and potentially provide Rail Services in competition with PN.

[1422] Now the ACCC says that the difference between such a future and the future with the ART acquisition is that in the future without, there would be more competition than in the future with the ART acquisition. There would again be three competitors in the Interstate market(s) being Qube, PN and SCT, rather than just PN and SCT, although query the relevant timeframe for entry in respect of Qube.

[1423] Further, on the ACCC’s case, Qube would not have an incentive to discriminate or foreclose a potential new entrant in the Interstate market(s) for the foreseeable future because it would not be profitable for it to do in circumstances where, as the new entrant operating on a smaller scale than PN, it would need to maximise revenue from PN’s use of the BMUT in order to underwrite its new entry.

[1424] Now one would need to compare such a scenario against the scenario of the ART acquisition with the undertaking. But on balance I am inclined to the view that there would be more of a chance of competition in the scenario without the ART acquisition and without the TSS.

[1425] But as I say, this is all a hypothetical thought experiment as the future without the ART acquisition includes the TSS with accordingly no entry by Qube.

[1426] In summary, and because the future with the ART acquisition includes the undertaking which removes any significant ability of PN to discriminate, the ACCC has not made out its s 50 case. Let me now turn to discuss the undertaking in more detail.

### PROPOSED UNDERTAKING

[1427] PN has offered to the Court an undertaking that it says would prevent it from being able to engage in discriminatory conduct of the type the ACCC hypothesises would occur in the world with the ART acquisition. I have annexed the form of this undertaking to these reasons (the Undertaking). This adapts the form of the proposed s 87B undertaking previously offered to the ACCC and rejected by it.

[1428] Before getting into the detail of the terms and adequacy of the Undertaking I should make the following observations.

[1429] First, the ACCC contended that I have a broad discretion to make orders under s 23 of the Federal Court of Australia Act 1976 (Cth), but there is a real question as to my power to impose monitoring and enforcement obligations on the ACCC in relation to an undertaking accepted by the Court, as this would go beyond the ACCC’s statutory powers. The ACCC is established under Part II of the CCA. It has statutory powers as provided by the specific provisions of the CCA and other Commonwealth legislation. Under s 87B(1) the ACCC has the discretionary power to accept an undertaking in connection with a matter in which it has powers or functions under the CCA and to apply to the Court for orders in relation to that undertaking. This is the only express statutory power that the ACCC has to accept an undertaking in connection with a matter arising under s 45 or s 50. Now as I have said, the ACCC previously considered an undertaking offered by PN under s 87B on similar terms to the Undertaking. But it declined to exercise its discretionary power to accept that undertaking pursuant to s 87B.

[1430] But the short answer to the ACCC’s point is that the Undertaking does not impose any monitoring or enforcement obligations on the ACCC.

[1431] Second, the ACCC contended that PN was seeking an advisory opinion. Now when the Undertaking was originally offered to me it was put on a conditional basis. So, PN said that it was only to be offered if I found that the ART acquisition otherwise contravened s 50. But this was not satisfactory and the ACCC’s criticisms had some force. What in essence PN was seeking from me was first an advisory opinion as to whether the ART acquisition contravened s 50. If it did, PN then proposed to give the Undertaking. And if I then accepted the Undertaking, then there would be no s 50 contravention. So, in substance, the first step in this chain was an advisory opinion, as the ACCC rightly contended. I raised this matter with Mr Hutley SC, encouraging him to reconsider his position. After some resistance, PN offered the Undertaking unconditionally. This meant that the ACCC’s advisory opinion point fell away. It also meant that I could take the unconditional giving of the Undertaking into account as part of the future with the ART acquisition assuming that I was prepared to accept it, which I am.

[1432] Third, the ACCC submitted that the Undertaking is akin to asking the Court to monitor the intricate and detailed contractual relations between parties to a commercial agreement on an almost daily basis, and to be available to do so at a moment’s notice because of commercial imperatives requiring, at times, rapid responses. It said that this is a high burden to place on the Court, which should be reluctant to undertake such obligations.

[1433] My response to this is: not really. There are a few points to make. A similar submission was made by the ACCC to French J in AGL and rejected by his Honour. I see no good reason not to follow his Honour’s approach on this aspect. Further, the Court is not monitoring anything as such. The Undertaking and PN’s behaviour will be observed by market participants and in one sense monitored by them; further, there is the role of the expert(s) and auditor under the Undertaking. If anyone seeks to enforce any breach of the Undertaking, then of course that will be a matter for the Court. Moreover, if there is Court enforcement, this can in one sense be more direct than proceeding through the enforcement of a s 87B undertaking. Moreover, the Court’s powers to impose relevant sanctions for contempt are broad, carrying with them significant deterrence effect such as to realistically discipline behaviour both of PN and any officers or employees who may have potential accessorial exposure.

[1434] Fourth, the ACCC led considerable evidence concerning perceived deficiencies with the Undertaking. I should say that I have carefully considered Dr Kuypers’ detailed criticisms of the prior proposed s 87B undertaking and those too of Mr Nacey, which criticisms I take to also apply to the Undertaking. But such criticisms are underwhelming so far as I am concerned.

[1435] Their evidence is only opinion evidence at best and in most respects amounts to speculation.

[1436] Further, these opinions hardly involve any scientific or technical complexity. I am well able to form my own commercial views based upon the primary evidence that I have heard. If there is any contentious subject matter where I ought to defer to expert opinion evidence, this is not one of them.

[1437] Further, Mr Nacey’s views have been refracted through Qube’s strategic lens to the ART acquisition and its interests in defeating it such that the line of his views is considerably deflected from a truly straight line trajectory.

[1438] Further, Dr Kuypers’ opinions on this aspect are entitled to respect. But when closely analysed, many of his criticisms of the Undertaking are flimsy or peripheral or both. I will discuss the relevant criticisms made by the ACCC based on Dr Kuypers’ evidence in a moment.

[1439] Fifth, at the end of the trial the ACCC appeared to have been caught by surprise by PN’s unconditional offering of the Undertaking. But nothing turns on this. It has had adequate opportunity to deal with the substance of the Undertaking, which in my view it has done through detailed submissions and evidence concerning the earlier proposed s 87B undertaking(s).

[1440] Let me now deal with the principal elements of the Undertaking before getting into the ACCC’s more detailed criticisms.

#### (a) The elements of the Undertaking

[1441] It is appropriate to highlight the following matters.

###### Non-discrimination obligations

[1442] First, cl 5.1 of the Undertaking requires that in owning, managing or operating the ART, PN comply with various “Access Conditions”, defined as the conditions listed in Sch 1 which include the following matters.

[1443] PN must offer “Terminal Services” to any “Applicant” on terms no less favourable than those it supplies to any other “Terminal User”, including itself (cl 2(a)(i)). “Terminal Services” are defined to include loading and unloading of containers, storage of containers, movement of containers, locomotive provisioning, train scheduling, marshalling or shunting and any other associated services. “Applicant” means any person or other “Terminal User” that has submitted to PN a bona fide application seeking Terminal Services. “Terminal User” is defined to include a “Rail Haulage Operator” using the “Terminal”, defined as the ART comprised of the BMUT and the Queensland Terminal. “Rail Haulage Operator” is defined as an entity that operates trains for the purposes of transporting goods, including containerised freight by rail. Therefore, Terminal User includes PN.

[1444] PN must not discriminate between Applicants and Terminal Users in offering and providing Terminal Services (cl 2(a)(ii)).

[1445] PN must not engage in conduct that prevents or hinders a Terminal User conducting its business using Terminal Services supplied under a “Terminal Services Agreement” (as defined), including the supply of any services by the Terminal User to third parties (cl 2(a)(iii)).
