[1446] PN must not engage in conduct in relation to its management or operation of the ART that prevents or hinders a third party acquiring any services from, or supplying any services to, a Terminal User (cl 2(a)(iv)).

[1447] In addition, the Undertaking expressly prohibits PN from engaging in discriminatory conduct in respect of storage of containers (see cls 5.3(a) to 5.3(c)), discriminatory conduct between users of the Terminal of the same class (cl 5.3(d)) and discriminatory conduct in favour of PN in the allocation of train schedules or yard space at the Terminal (cl 5.3(e)).

[1448] Accordingly, the ACCC’s hypothesised discriminatory conduct would be prohibited by the Undertaking, and any breach of the Undertaking would be expected to attract contempt proceedings.

###### Pricing

[1449] Second, the Undertaking imposes a cap on the access fees that PN can charge until 30 June 2019 (cl 5.2 and Sch 1, cl 5(b)). Thereafter, if or when PN proposed to increase its prices, any user could object to the proposed price increase and refer the matter to an “Independent Price Expert” being the person appointed under cl 10. The Independent Price Expert determines whether the increase is “reasonable and appropriate” having regard to principles listed in Sch 4, cl 3.4. Those principles include the interests of all users of the Terminal Services for which the proposed charges relate, including the extent to which the proposed charges reflect actual or likely use of the Terminal by different users, including use of facilities and equipment (Sch 4, cl 3.4(b)(vi)). Such principles also include the reasonableness and appropriateness of, and justification for, the existing charges for the supply of the Terminal Services (Sch 4, cl 3.4(b)(vii)).

[1450] As in any long-term contract that provides for the resolution of pricing disputes by a third party, this gives the access seeker considerable bargaining power in respect of access fees and the confidence that it would not be required to accept any fee increases that were not reasonable or appropriate.

###### Reporting requirements

[1451] Third, every six months PN must prepare and publish reports on its website that record its performance against key performance indicators (KPIs) for any services provided by PN to itself and any services provided to other Terminal Users (cl 8). Those KPIs include, inter alia:

  (a) whether trains are ready for departure on time, with the objective of 90% of trains departing the Terminal within 30 minutes of the scheduled departure time;

  (b) truck turnaround times, with the objective of trucks spending no more than 35 minutes on average at the Terminal picking up or delivering freight;

  (c) freight availability, with the objective of 80% of freight being available within one hour of the time that the train is made available to PN for unloading;

  (d) transaction times per container, with the objective that the time it takes to process a container from truck to ground, truck to train, train to truck and ground to truck is no more than 25 minutes on average;

  (e) load to train load plan, with the object of 80% of containers being loaded according to the operator’s train load plan; and

  (f) freight dwell time, with the objective of 95% of freight being picked up within the “free time”.

[1452] Therefore, material differences between the services that PN supplies to itself and another user of the Terminal would have to be published by PN.

[1453] Now the ACCC says that the KPIs are incomplete because they do not measure matters such as how often terminal windows are cancelled or containers are damaged. But there is no evidence of any terminal window being cancelled at the ART for maintenance or any other reason. In Mr Graham Moore’s experience it has never happened. Further, containers are all inspected before leaving the Terminal, and any damaged containers are reported. There is an air of unreality to the ACCC’s criticisms.

###### Independent audit

[1454] Fourth, PN must appoint an “Independent Auditor” to report annually on its compliance with the Undertaking (cl 9.1). The Independent Auditor must, inter alia:

  (a) thoroughly audit PN’s compliance with the Undertaking (cl 9.7(b));

  (b) outline areas of uncertainty or ambiguity in the auditor’s interpretation of any obligations in the Undertaking (cl 9.7(c));

  (c) report any issues that arise in relation to PN’s compliance with the Undertaking (cl 9.5(d)(ii)); and

  (d) identify any recommendations to improve, inter-alia:

    (i) PN’s processes for reporting systems in relation to compliance with the Undertaking (cl 9.7(f)(iii));

    (ii) the KPIs in Sch 3 (cl 9.7(f)(iv)); and

    (iii) the requirements and obligations in the Undertaking to achieve the objectives of the Undertaking (cl 9.7(f)(v)).

[1455] PN is required to facilitate this process (cl 9.15(c)(i)). Accordingly, there would be regular, independent scrutiny of PN, which would ensure that PN was complying with its obligation not to discriminate against any user of the ART.

###### Capacity allocation

[1456] Fifth, the Undertaking provides that PN is not responsible for determining capacity allocation. If PN receives a request for access to the ART, and has not entered into a terminal services agreement with any other user at the time, it would have to appoint an “Independent Expert” to prepare a “capacity allocation protocol” (CAP) that will apply to the ART (cl 6.1). The Independent Expert’s CAP must not permit PN to favour any one user (including itself) over another (cl 6.3). And PN must then allocate capacity in accordance with that protocol (cl 6.2).

[1457] Now the ACCC says, for example, that:

  (a) the obligation only applies in respect of the first user to access the ART;

  (b) the process for developing a CAP can take considerable time (according to Dr Kuypers, up to 289 days is possible); and

  (c) the Undertaking gives limited guidance to the Independent Expert for the preparation of the CAP.

[1458] But the obligation to prepare a CAP only applies in respect of the first user to access the ART because, once a CAP is in place, any Terminal User or PN can apply to have it varied (see cl 6.13). In other words, once a CAP is in place, there is no need for a second one to be created. The first is simply amended if necessary in light of any later access applications.

[1459] Further, Mr Nacey agreed that it takes years to plan for and commence supply of a new intermodal rail service. In that context, even if the ACCC were correct about the maximum possible time required to prepare a CAP, that would not likely prevent or impede successful entry.

[1460] Further, detailed guidance about how to prepare the CAP is not required. The Undertaking expressly requires that the expert is independent and suitably qualified and does not favour PN over any other user. In those circumstances, it is not likely that any reasonable access seeker would be deterred from entry by this process.

###### Capacity expansion

[1461] Sixth, there is a detailed process by which Applicants and Terminal Users can, in effect, request that PN expand the capacity of the Terminal and, if PN does not agree, request an Independent Expert to require that PN expand the capacity of the Terminal (see cls 6.22 to 6.44).

[1462] Now Mr Nacey was critical of this process and said that there was no obligation on PN to increase the capacity on its own, it would take up to 12 to 18 months to determine whether the expansion works were going to occur and PN would not be obliged to pay for the expansion works.

[1463] But new entry would likely occur on a relatively small scale and there is ample existing capacity to support such entry. Mr Graham Moore estimates that the annual capacity of the BMUT is approximately 350,000 TEUs, that there is currently 70,000 TEUs of excess capacity and that there will be excess capacity until 2025/2026, even without undertaking the many efficiency improvements that are available. Further, it takes years to plan for and commence supply of a new intermodal rail service, and even longer before any new service would reach a scale that would require an expansion of capacity at the BMUT. So even if the lead time for expanding capacity at the BMUT is 12 to 18 months, that would not prevent or impede successful entry. Further, there is no evidence about the likely costs of expansion works or the likely return that an entrant or existing service provider would earn on investment in those works. The notion that they would provide a material barrier to entry is speculative.

#### (b) The ACCC’s more detailed criticisms

[1464] The ACCC says that the unconditional offer of the Undertaking does not address the fundamental flaws inherent in the Undertaking.

[1465] The ACCC says that the Undertaking would be completely ineffectual due to deficiencies in the core obligations on PN, as well as inadequate and unworkable monitoring and compliance mechanisms. It says that access undertakings require stringent monitoring and enforcement by well-resourced and experienced regulators, and should be subject to periodic review and revision. It is said that it is not possible to replicate and enforce an effective access regime through an undertaking to the Court. Accordingly, it says that the Undertaking should not be accepted in an attempt to address the competition concerns arising from the major structural change that will result from the ART acquisition. It will be apparent from what I have said earlier that I would reject that submission. Indeed, the ACCC is overplaying its hand by suggesting (as I understood its submission) that it has unfettered control and dominance over whether an access undertaking should be accepted, that is, that such an undertaking can only be dealt with under s 87B but not otherwise under a mechanism exogenous to s 87B and not involving the ACCC.

[1466] The ACCC says that the most significant issue is that PN’s obligations are not subject to active independent monitoring and enforcement. It says that critical independent roles which are supposed to ensure PN’s compliance with the Undertaking are to be filled by PN appointed third parties. Additionally, various protections are not included such as:

  (a) a process which applies if PN does not take steps to appoint an auditor;

  (b) prohibition of specific ‘non-independent’ relationships with the auditor;

  (c) oversight of the compliance Audit Report (see cl 9.7); under the Undertaking it is only provided to PN and the auditor, so third parties will not be aware of the report; and

  (d) a process to review the Undertaking over time; the requirements will be static for the term of the Undertaking and will not respond to changes in industry circumstances over time other than those that PN has an incentive to change by applying to the Court to suit its own interests.

[1467] The ACCC refers to Mr Nacey’s evidence in relation to the originally offered s 87B undertaking and more generally any access undertaking of any kind to the effect that it would create such significant risks that he would not recommend that Qube establish a business supplying Rail Services in the future with the ART acquisition with such an undertaking. I have referred to Mr Nacey’s evidence earlier. As I say, it is unsurprising given Qube’s strategic objectives.

[1468] Further, the ACCC says that the Undertaking will be ineffectual in addressing the ability and incentive of PN to discriminate, as well as new entrants’ perception of that ability and incentive. It says that the Undertaking cannot be relied on to effectively alter the future state of competition with the ART acquisition. In the future with the ART acquisition, and even with the Undertaking, barriers to entry would be materially higher and new entry into the Interstate market(s) would be unlikely, whether by Qube or any other new entrant.

[1469] Further, in assessing whether the ART acquisition is likely to have the effect of substantially lessening competition in breach of s 50, the ACCC says that the future with is to be compared to the future state of competition without the ART acquisition. The ACCC says that this future without will be without the ART acquisition and the Undertaking, but with or without the TSS, depending on my findings in respect of the s 45 case concerning the TSS. As I have said, the future without will be with the TSS as there is no s 45 breach.

[1470] The ACCC says that in a future without the ART acquisition and the Undertaking, and also without the TSS if it is found to contravene s 45, the ART would either be retained by Aurizon or sold to Qube or another non-vertically integrated third party. Aurizon or any such purchaser would have an incentive to encourage new rail operators to use the BMUT as competitors to PN and barriers to entry into the Interstate market(s) would be materially lower. In these circumstances, the threat of new entry for the supply of North-South and/or East-West Rail Services would constrain PN’s supply of Rail Services. Further, in this future, there is a real chance that Qube would commence supplying North-South and/or East-West Rail Services in competition with PN. Accordingly, the ACCC says that the ART acquisition would be likely to have the effect of substantially lessening competition and the Undertaking will not affect this conclusion. Now this scenario can be rejected as the future without does include the TSS, and Qube has said that it will not enter with the TSS.

[1471] Further, the ACCC says that in the scenario where the TSS is held not to contravene s 45, the future without will be without the ART acquisition and Undertaking but with the TSS. But it says that to reach this conclusion regarding the TSS case, I must have found that the TSS does not increase barriers to entry because it does not confer a material degree of operational control on PN, and that the owner of the ART would retain and exercise (or threaten to exercise) significant control over terminal operations sufficient to constrain PN’s ability to discriminate against competitors seeking to use the BMUT. It says that if, contrary to its submissions regarding the TSS, there is a finding regarding the interpretation and limits of the TSS, this may negate or reduce the perception that under the TSS PN has the ability and incentive to discriminate against new entrants seeking access to the ART. But as I have said, even if it would, Qube has said that it will not enter with the TSS. It was quite definitive on at least that part of its strategy and evidence.

[1472] In these circumstances, so the ACCC says, the comparison will be between: (a) a future with the ART acquisition which delivers control and the ability and incentive to discriminate despite the Undertaking and thereby materially increases barriers to entry; and (b) a future without the ART acquisition where the TSS is found not to deliver operational control or increase barriers to entry, and where an alternative owner of the ART with an incentive to facilitate entry to increase volumes at the ART is responsible for determining terminal access conditions and charges for a new entrant. Again, the ACCC says that the ART acquisition would be likely to have the effect of substantially lessening competition in the relevant markets.

[1473] Let me now elaborate further on specific complaints made by the ACCC concerning the Undertaking and my evaluation thereof.

###### The Undertaking would not change PN’s underlying incentives

[1474] The ACCC says that because the Undertaking imposes obligations on PN without altering the underlying market structure, PN is required to operate in a manner inconsistent with its own profit maximising incentives. This means that the Undertaking’s effectiveness is dependent on its ability to force PN to act contrary to its own interests.

[1475] Now the ACCC accepts that where the Undertaking imposes an obligation on PN that is certain and not discretionary, then it may be effective even despite PN’s contrary incentives. But where the obligation is ambiguous, or where an exemption limits the scope of a requirement imposed on PN, PN will seek to maximise its profits and will act consistently with its own self-interest. The ACCC says that the Undertaking contains significant discretionary matters which cannot be avoided. Accordingly, PN would be incentivised to do everything in its power to frustrate the access sought by competing suppliers of Rail Services, without breaching the terms of the Undertaking.

[1476] The ACCC says that significantly, the obligations on PN under the Undertaking either fail to address or give PN discretion over issues that are of critical importance to a potential competing third party rail operator seeking access to the ART. And PN has a clear incentive to exploit these shortcomings.

[1477] But as to this, the ACCC has considerably exaggerated the so called “significant discretionary matters which cannot be avoided”, as is apparent from my specific discussion of relevant provisions of the Undertaking. Further, PN has an incentive to operate the ART in an efficient manner which maximises the throughput of the ART.

[1478] Further, any breach of the Undertaking can attract the sanction of contempt of court, which potential consequence would discipline PN’s profit maximising incentives.

###### Limitations in the scope of the Undertaking

[1479] The ACCC says that cl 5.1 of the Undertaking is the main clause imposing non-discrimination obligations on PN. It states that “Pacific National must in owning, managing or operating the Terminal comply with the Access Conditions set out in Schedule 1”.

[1480] Schedule 1 provides various restrictions on the manner in which PN may offer and provide Terminal Services. Terminal Services are defined so as to be confined to services provided within the Terminal. As I have said, Terminal is defined at cl 17 of the Undertaking to mean “the Acacia Ridge Intermodal Terminal, comprised of the BMUT and the Queensland Terminal”, and BMUT is defined to mean “the Brisbane Multi-User Terminal, being the standard gauge terminal located at Acacia Ridge”.

[1481] The ACCC says that as those definitions are not precise, PN is acquiring more than the (defined) BMUT and the Queensland Terminal in its proposed acquisition of the ART. Significantly, it is not clear that the marshalling yard is part of either the Queensland Terminal or the BMUT in these definitions, and may therefore be outside the scope of the Undertaking.

[1482] The ACCC says that access to the marshalling yard is essential in order for a rail operator to use the BMUT. Access to the yards are required in order for trains to be shunted and stored whilst waiting to be loaded, unloaded, or released onto the network, particularly if assigned to New Crane Rd No. 1 and New Crane Rd No. 2. Steel trains need to move through the ART on standard gauge to be unloaded at BlueScope’s facility. In order for a new entrant to be able to provide steel services, it must be able to use the marshalling yard.

[1483] Further, the ACCC says that the concept of Terminal Services does not clearly include the allocation of tracks within BMUT. Even if the allocation of tracks within BMUT for loading and unloading (and in particular, the allocation of A Track as opposed to New Crane Rd No. 1 and New Crane Rd No. 2) is not within the control of PN under the PN TSS, it would be within the control of PN as the owner of the ART. The Undertaking does not clearly impose non-discriminatory obligations upon PN about allocation of tracks.

[1484] I would reject these arguments.

[1485] It is clear that the marshalling yard forms part of the Acacia Ridge Intermodal Terminal. This is apparent from the inclusion of “marshalling” as a Terminal Service at paragraph (f) of the definition of Terminal Services.

[1486] Further, Sch 1, cl 5(b) includes specific charges for the service of “Access to the Marshalling Yard”. PN says that this would be confirmed by the pro forma terminal services agreement to be published on PN’s website. So, an access seeker would be provided access to the marshalling yard in addition to the BMUT and/or Queensland Terminal.

[1487] Further, it is true that “allocation of tracks” is not expressly stated to be a Terminal Service in the Undertaking. However, it is likely to at least be an associated right, benefit or privilege necessary or desirable to permit a Terminal User to receive all or part of the services offered by PN at the Terminal, or to fall within paragraph (g), being “any other services associated with, ancillary to, or provided in connection with the services specified in (a) to (f)”. Therefore, it, just as with marshalling, would be covered by the non-discrimination obligations, for example, in Sch 1, cl 2(a).

###### No guarantee of access to the BMUT

[1488] The ACCC says that the Undertaking does not provide any guarantee that a third party who seeks to use the BMUT will in fact be granted access by PN.

[1489] But Sch 1, cl 4(b) requires PN to negotiate in good faith for the provision of Terminal Services. Schedule 1, cl 4(c) then requires PN to provide an unsuccessful Applicant with a statement of reasons. Such reasons would reasonably be expected to include matters such as that there is insufficient terminal capacity to accommodate the access seeker’s access request or PN considered that the access seeker was not solvent or would not be able to meet its potential liabilities under a terminal services agreement.

[1490] Further, on the ACCC’s submission PN would be obliged to grant access to an access seeker in the absence of a proper and reasonable basis for its application.

[1491] I agree with PN that a guarantee of the kind suggested by the ACCC would be an unrealistic requirement for any terminal owner.

###### Terms of access under the Undertaking may still be discriminatory

[1492] The ACCC says that even if access to the BMUT was granted, the Undertaking does not provide a third party with any assurance that access would be granted on non-discriminatory terms.

[1493] The main non-discrimination obligations in the Undertaking are contained in cl 5 and in Sch 1. However, the ACCC says that key clauses in Sch 1 either provide discretion to PN as to how it must comply with the requirements or expressly allow for PN to discriminate against third parties. In particular:

  (a) Clause 2(a)(i) of Sch 1 provides that “Pacific National must offer Terminal Services to an Applicant on terms no less favourable than terms for Terminal Services offered to another Terminal User.” “No less favourable” does not mean the same terms. Aside from specific examples provided in cl 5.3, the Undertaking provides no basis upon which the terms offered to an Applicant are to be compared to those of another Terminal User.

  (b) Clause 3(d) of Sch 1 provides that “Pacific National must ensure that every Applicant can demonstrate, to Pacific National’s reasonable satisfaction, that the Applicant … has in place appropriate occupational health and safety standards and rail safety standards.” Having safety as an eligibility criterion is appropriate, however this clause allows PN discretion to decide (to its reasonable satisfaction) whether an Applicant has appropriate safety standards. PN could use this criterion to delay entry by not accepting an application on safety grounds, thereby forcing the Applicant to trigger the non-price dispute mechanism. Alternatively, it could use this clause to raise its potential rival’s costs by imposing higher safety-related standards on another user than it applied to itself.

  (c) Clause 6(a) of Sch 1 provides that “It is acknowledged that separate to the Undertaking, Pacific National is required to also comply with Rail Regulations.” Dr Kuypers has explained that PN’s ability to rely on rail regulations in the undertaking allows it to impose additional safety requirements (causing additional cost and delay) on its competitors, without breaching the undertaking.

  (d) Other obligations in Sch 1 impose behavioural standards on PN that nonetheless leave PN with significant discretion as to how it behaves, such as to “negotiate in good faith for the provision of Terminal Services”, “use all reasonable endeavours to maximise the use of the rail network”, and “use its best endeavours to accommodate Trains that present at the Terminal before or after the designated arrival time…”

[1494] Each of the clauses identified above provide PN with discretion as to how it operates the ART:

  (a) to offer different terms to an Applicant based on the Applicant’s unique circumstances;

  (b) with respect to the safety requirements imposed on a future Applicant; or

  (c) in dealing with the requirements of trains that arrive at the ART before or after their designated arrival time.

[1495] The ACCC says that operating the ART for multiple terminal users is a complex process, and an operator of the ART requires some level of discretion to be able to effectively deal with unforeseen circumstances, or conflicts between objectives. The complexity of the operations of the ART, and the need to exercise discretion on a regular basis in operational decisions provides the opportunity for PN to operate it in a discriminatory manner. This shows the inherent inability of any behavioural undertaking to remedy the anti-competitive effects of the ART acquisition. The consequence of the Undertaking allowing PN operational flexibility to deal with complex scenarios is that it provides PN with the ability to discriminate without breaching the Undertaking.

[1496] Further, the Undertaking does not contain detailed operational constraints of the type which might be expected to be included in such an undertaking to limit discriminatory treatment of third parties, and which are included in the access arrangement for PN’s South Dynon terminal. In particular, the Undertaking does not include:

  (a) any standard form terminal services agreement or other guidance as to the terms on which a third party could expect to be required under a terminal services agreement; or

  (b) a terminal protocol governing the manner in which PN will operate the ART.

[1497] The ACCC says that the failure to include these is likely to increase the scope for discrimination, and the time and expense involved in a third party seeking to raise concerns regarding discriminatory treatment.

[1498] But as to the above concerns, the requirement that PN offer Terminal Services on terms that are “no less favourable” than the terms offered to another Terminal User is appropriate. PN should not be required to provide access to all Terminal Users on identical terms.

[1499] Further, as the owner of the Terminal, it is appropriate that PN require an access seeker to have in place appropriate health and safety standards. It is not commercially realistic to suggest that PN would seek to impose additional safety obligations on an access seeker merely to increase its costs or to delay the granting of access to an access seeker to the Terminal. Moreover, an access seeker could commence a dispute under the non-price dispute resolution process if it suspected that PN was acting for a collateral purpose.

[1500] Further, cl 6(a) of Sch 1 of the Undertaking states only that PN is required to comply with “Rail Regulations” (as defined). It does not permit it to unilaterally impose additional safety obligations on any other party.

[1501] Further, the requirements under the Undertaking for PN to “negotiate in good faith”, “use all reasonable endeavours” and to “use best endeavours” are familiar phrases with well accepted meanings.

[1502] Further, it is wrong to suggest that the Undertaking does not contain a standard form terminal services agreement. Schedule 1, cl 4(a)(iii) imposes an obligation on PN to publish on its website the terms and conditions on which Terminal Services are offered, that is, a pro-forma terminal services agreement.

[1503] Further, it would be inappropriate for PN to prepare a terminal protocol setting out how it would operate the Terminal in advance of it owning the Terminal. Such a protocol can only be developed after PN is operating within the Terminal.

[1504] I agree with PN that the ACCC’s criticisms in respect of the ‘discretionary’ terms of the Undertaking are of secondary significance.

###### The Undertaking is not clear how capacity would be reallocated to a new entrant

[1505] The ACCC says that the Undertaking does not adequately address:

  (a) how capacity at BMUT would be allocated to a new entrant;

  (b) certainty as to what capacity is likely exist at the BMUT in future; or

  (c) what obligations PN would have to expand the capacity of the BMUT in order to accommodate a new entrant’s use of the BMUT.

[1506] The ACCC says that the Undertaking does not define “capacity”, creating immediate scope for PN to limit the manner in which any obligations imposed on it are applied in practice to the BMUT. Putting that to one side, the ACCC says that the allocation of existing capacity at the BMUT under the Undertaking is dealt with by way of a CAP, defined above. But this mechanism is deficient for two reasons: first, a CAP’s existence depends upon a series of steps being undertaken, the first of which is PN determining that a new entrant has made a bona fide request for access; and second, there is no specified content of a CAP, and no template or standard form CAP provided, leaving a new entrant with only limited (and itself problematic) guidance on what the CAP may look like.

[1507] The ACCC says that for a CAP to be prepared, PN must first determine that a new entrant’s request is bona fide. But the Undertaking provides no guidance on how a new entrant would establish its bona fides, or what factors PN must take into account when making such a determination. It is unclear whether a potential new entrant whose request for access was not considered bona fide would meet the definition of “Dispute Applicant”, which appears again to be in PN’s discretion necessary for it to engage the non-price dispute resolution process in an effort to challenge that determination.

[1508] The ACCC refers to the fact that should the new entrant’s request for access be considered bona fide by PN, an Independent Expert is appointed to prepare a CAP. Clauses 6.4 to 6.12 of the Undertaking set out a process for the preparation of the CAP, which is to take up to 40 business days, following the appointment of the Independent Expert. But the ACCC says that the Undertaking is silent on the timeframe by which PN must then apply the CAP to the BMUT.

[1509] The ACCC says that the evidence of Dr Kuypers is that a CAP is “the document which should deal with key issues” regarding the allocation of terminal windows, the characteristics of which “determine not only customer attractiveness but…are also a key driver of operational efficiency and costs”. Nevertheless, the Undertaking provides no form of CAP, and new entrants would not know how capacity would be allocated at the BMUT, or whether sufficient capacity would be allocated to them, until the preparation of the CAP, should such preparation even occur.

[1510] For example, they would not know how much existing and future capacity was reserved for PN, or the nature, quality or priority of capacity that might be made available to them. The ACCC says that the Undertaking provides scope for the CAP to permit capacity to be allocated in a way that discriminates in favour of PN and against third parties, for example, to enable capacity to be reserved to protect PN’s customers against the consequences of PN’s trains running late, rather than being allocated to a third party.

[1511] The ACCC says that the only guidance provided by the Undertaking on what may be expected in the CAP appears to afford scope for PN to favour its own services over those provided to a third party. The CAP must not permit PN to favour itself “except to the extent required…having regard to third party operational considerations”. But that phrase is undefined. It could include considerations of PN customers (who may be third parties for this purpose) – for example, if the allocation of a terminal window to another user would limit PN’s ability to service its train if running late, and thereby negatively affect its customers’ ability to collect freight at the nominated time, PN would appear to be entitled to favour its own services over the other user’s services. There is otherwise no relevant guidance on what the Independent Expert must take into account when preparing the CAP.

[1512] The ACCC says that this issue is more than theoretical. It says that although PN asserts that it is incentivised to grant access, history shows that Aurizon previously refused SCT access to the BMUT on the basis that there was no capacity.

[1513] I reject these criticisms.

[1514] As to the above submissions, the requirement that PN receive a bona fide access application is not unusual. It ensures that real and not trivial applications are acted upon.

[1515] Further, the CAP is to be prepared by an Independent Expert, being a person with qualifications and experience necessary to carry out the functions required under cl 6.

[1516] Further, once a CAP has been finalised by the Independent Expert, PN would be required to immediately apply it at the Terminal. Clause 6.2 of the Undertaking imposes an express obligation to publish the CAP on its website and to allocate capacity at the Terminal in accordance with the CAP.

[1517] Further, there is no need for a CAP until such time as a new access seeker starts operating services at the Terminal. Therefore, the requirement for a CAP to be prepared only arises once a bona fide access application is received. It would be difficult to identify every matter that may be relevant to such a protocol in advance. The Independent Expert is required to consult the CAP. Therefore, any access seeker will be provided with a copy of the draft CAP and will be able to provide comments to the Independent Expert on the draft CAP.

[1518] Further, it is not correct to assert that the Undertaking provides scope for the CAP to permit capacity to be allocated in a discriminatory manner that favours PN. The CAP will be prepared by an Independent Expert. PN will be in no different a position than an access seeker insofar as PN can provide comments on the draft of the CAP to the Independent Expert. Further, cl 6.3 of the Undertaking provides that the CAP must not permit PN to favour itself or another person over any other person.

###### The Undertaking gives no certainty that capacity will be expanded to accommodate a new entrant

[1519] The ACCC says that at some point, potentially in the next 3 to 5 years, expansion would likely be required in order to accommodate a new entrant at the BMUT as it expands its operations. However, the absence of an obligation on PN to accommodate third party demand in the Undertaking means that if such expansion or other works were required, the third party would have to “force” those works to be undertaken, at its own cost, in circumstances where PN had an incentive to prevent or impede the third party from using the BMUT.

[1520] The ACCC says that no previous examples have been identified of any rail terminal or infrastructure in Australia being expanded to facilitate third party use without the terminal owner’s support. Further, it says that if a third party did seek to “force” such an expansion, it would face the following challenges:

  (a) To engage the capacity expansion clause of the Undertaking, the third party must either be an existing Terminal User, or be deemed by PN to have submitted a bona fide application seeking Terminal Services, in respect of which there are problems.

  (b) The process involved in requiring PN to expand the ART is extremely time consuming, and could take in excess of 200 business days to complete.

  (c) PN is not required to undertake any expansion:

    (i) which would require it to acquire or make use of third party land, notwithstanding that use of such land may well be required in order to cost-effectively expand the ART; or

    (ii) if, despite its reasonable endeavours, it is unable to obtain necessary consents and approvals within the relevant time frame, in which case PN can cease any expansion process without reimbursing the third party’s costs.

[1521] Importantly, so the ACCC says, PN is under no obligation to pay the costs associated with expanding the ART. If a third party does not agree with PN’s cost allocation, it must raise a specific dispute on this issue, which would involve further time and cost. The Undertaking requires the allocation of costs of the works to be agreed between PN and the third party. In circumstances where the third party and PN do not agree on the allocation of costs for the works, the allocation may be determined by the Independent Price Expert (the person appointed under cl 10). However, the Independent Price Expert is not required to undertake a de novo assessment of the allocation of costs. Instead, the Independent Price Expert begins with PN’s proposed allocation of costs (and does not consider the third party’s proposed allocation of costs) and makes a determination on whether that is reasonable and appropriate. So, according to the ACCC, such a starting point necessarily advantages PN’s position.

[1522] Further, the ACCC says that the Undertaking provides that should an Independent Price Expert be engaged, and the scope of the works, or the estimated cost of the works, determined by the Independent Price Expert differs from PN’s scope or estimated cost by less than 10%, PN is not required to undertake the works in the manner determined by the Independent Price Expert. However, this clause does not require PN to actually carry out the works, notwithstanding the determination of the Independent Price Expert.

[1523] As a result of these matters, the ACCC says that there is no certainty that any expansion works required to accommodate a third party would occur. And even if they did, there would be a delay of at least 12 to 18 months to determine whether they would occur, and no certainty about how long the construction process would take. The ACCC says that Mr Nacey gave evidence that he doubts whether Qube would be able to obtain the necessary customer support to enter the market with such a long lead time.

[1524] I reject the ACCC’s criticisms.

[1525] It is unlikely that a capacity expansion will be required in the next 3 to 5 years. Mr Graham Moore gave evidence that at the BMUT there is currently 70,000 TEUs of excess annual capacity and that some form of excess capacity will remain until 2025/2026, based on an assumption as to the site’s then current usage, operating model, customer offer and configuration. And after 5 years, other terminals may come on stream.

[1526] Further, the ACCC assumes that only a new access seeker would require a capacity expansion in the future. Hence, PN would not be inclined to support any future capacity expansion. But this is an erroneous assumption. If demand for PN’s rail linehaul services increased, then it too may require a capacity expansion in the future. Moreover, the fact that terminals have not previously been expanded for third party use without owner support does not demonstrate anything other than the fact that there are likely to be good commercial incentives for a terminal owner to expand if there is a willing entrant asking them to do so.

[1527] In any case, the Undertaking provides a clear process, supported by the presence of an Independent Expert, for the expansion of capacity at the ART.

[1528] Further, it is reasonable that PN first receive a bona fide access application prior to it proceeding with the capacity expansion process outlined in the Undertaking.

[1529] Further, even if capacity expansion is a time consuming process, Mr Nacey stated in evidence that it takes years to plan for and commence supply of a new intermodal rail service. A lead time of 12 to 18 months for capacity expansion would not prevent or impede successful entry.

[1530] Further, it is reasonable that PN should not be required to acquire or obtain an interest in neighbouring land in order for it to expand the capacity of the Terminal (cl 6.43(b)). Similarly, PN should not be held to account, despite doing everything within its power, for decisions made or not made by third parties such as local governments, regarding any development or similar application made to facilitate a capacity expansion at the Terminal (cl 6.43(c)).

[1531] Further, the ACCC’s assertions that PN has no obligation to pay the costs associated with expanding the ART is a distorted reflection of the Undertaking. PN need not pay the costs associated with undertaking the “Feasibility Assessment” (cl 6.34). Otherwise, the Undertaking provides for PN and an access seeker to reach agreement between themselves on the allocation of costs for undertaking a capacity expansion (cl 6.39). If PN and an access seeker cannot agree on the allocation of costs, then the Independent Price Expert may determine the issue. It is not correct that the Independent Price Expert is not required to consider an access seeker’s proposed allocation of costs. The “Expansion Dispute Notice” is required to set out both PN’s and the access seeker’s alternative proposed allocation of costs (Sch 4, cl 6.1(b)). The Independent Price Expert is required to determine whether PN’s proposed allocation of costs is reasonable and appropriate, having regard to the factors in cl 6.32 (Sch 4, cl 6.3(a)(i)). However, in making his determination the Independent Price Expert may “accept, reject or vary” PN’s proposed cost allocation (Sch 4, cl 6.3(a)(ii)). Nothing prevents the Independent Price Expert from making a determination that varies PN’s proposed cost allocation in such a way that it more closely resembles the access seeker’s proposed cost allocation.

[1532] Further, there is nothing unusual about an entrant, for the benefit of whom a piece of infrastructure is being expanded, being required to share the cost of that expansion.

[1533] Further, in relation to cl 6.42 of the Undertaking, this clause merely provides that PN is not required to undertake any “Works”, defined as any construction of improvements to the Terminal, “in the manner determined by the Independent Expert” where the difference in either the scope or estimated cost of the works when compared with the scope determined by PN is less than 10%. PN, however, is still required by cl 6.41(a) of the Undertaking to undertake the Works. The effect of cl 6.42 is to permit PN to undertake the Works in its preferred manner.

[1534] Further, as Aurizon points out, there may be some benefit arising from the Undertaking which would not be present if Aurizon retained ownership of the ART, that is, that a capacity enhancement regime would exist, whereas that would not otherwise be the case. I reject the assertion of the ACCC that this perceived benefit is illusory.

###### The Undertaking allows for price discrimination against a new entrant

[1535] The ACCC says that the Undertaking contains a mechanism by which charges for Terminal Services are published and may be disputed, but the mechanism is deficient in providing a new entrant non-discriminatory terms of access for at least the following reasons.

[1536] First, the price dispute mechanism is engaged when the Independent Price Expert receives an objection notice from a Dispute Applicant. If no objection notice is received, PN can increase charges without constraint. Given the likely lead time for any new entrant to meet the definition of Dispute Applicant (that is, having a genuine direct or indirect economic interest in the terms and conditions of use of the Terminal), may extend to several years, there is nothing in the Undertaking to stop PN from significantly increasing charges whilst no Dispute Applicant exists to a point at which seeking access to the BMUT is economically unviable on price alone.

[1537] Second, the price dispute mechanism applies to charges, which are fees payable to PN for a defined set of Terminal Services. These Terminal Services are services provided to rail operators, and as such, there is no mechanism in the Undertaking to limit or otherwise deal with fees or charges that PN may levy on other parties whose access to the Terminal may be necessary for a new entrant’s operation.

[1538] Third, the Undertaking provides a ‘floor’ charge by way of the initial charges. It is only future price increases that are subject to the Independent Price Expert’s (problematic) consideration, meaning that the starting price floor may already be inconsistent with the “relevant considerations” that the Independent Price Expert must have regard to when determining whether a charge is reasonable and appropriate.

[1539] Fourth, the Independent Price Expert is only required to determine whether PN’s proposed increase is reasonable and appropriate, having regard to a limited set of considerations. That is, it is not required to undertake a truly independent de novo assessment of prices, and its obligations carry the nebulous requirement of determining what is “reasonable and appropriate”. It relies on the information given to it by PN to make the assessment. It may be possible for PN to (for example) overstate terminal costs by allocating common costs with the effect of increasing the new entrant’s price of access.

[1540] Fifth, the Independent Price Expert cannot impose a charge lower than PN’s charges in the current year. As such, the price dispute mechanism contains what Dr Kuypers refers to as a “ratchet mechanism” by which prices can only go up, not down. The effect of this is that should PN’s marginal costs decrease (for example, with efficiencies from new technologies), there is no mechanism or obligations for charges to correspondingly decrease. A competitor of PN using the Terminal may therefore be paying terminal services costs to PN that are greater than the costs actually incurred by PN, placing the competitor at a competitive disadvantage.

[1541] Sixth, the Undertaking specifically allows for “multi-part pricing and price discrimination” where “the cost of providing the service is higher” or where doing so would “aid efficiency”. These principles, which the Independent Price Expert must have regard to, are not set out in detail. The evidence of Dr Kuypers is that the very nature of providing services to a third party rather than oneself has cost implications and might by that nature give rise to allowable charge discrimination. Further, the information asymmetry between PN’s, and its claims on costs and efficiencies, and the Independent Price Expert’s actual visibility on those costs and efficiencies gives no comfort to a new entrant that this principle would be applied fairly.

[1542] As to these submissions I would make the following points.

[1543] I agree with PN that it is erroneous to assert that PN can increase its access charges without constraint. PN must notify the Independent Auditor (the person appointed under cl 9) of any proposed price increase (Sch 4, cl 2.2(a)(ii)). The Independent Auditor may then request the Independent Price Expert to prepare a report regarding whether PN’s proposed price increase is reasonable and appropriate, having regard to defined considerations at Sch 4, cl 3.4 (cl 9.9). That report is to be prepared prior to when the proposed price increase would take effect. The report is provided to the Independent Auditor, the ACCC and PN. The Independent Price Expert may determine an alternative price if he/she forms the view that PN’s proposed price increase is not reasonable and appropriate, having regard to the considerations at Sch 4, cl 3.4 (Sch 4, cl 3.3(a)(i)). That alternative price then takes effect from the same date that the proposed price increase would have commenced.

[1544] Further, the pricing provisions in the Undertaking only concern the prices of Terminal Services. I agree with PN that there is no requirement for the Undertaking to be broadened so that it covers any fees or charges which might be levied by PN on other parties.

[1545] Further, there is no need for the initial access prices that PN has proposed to offer to be assessed. These prices reflect the arms-length prices that were previously negotiated by PN and Aurizon. In a sense, they already represent the market price for Terminal Services.

[1546] Further, the Independent Price Expert’s task is not simply to determine whether a proposed price increase is reasonable and appropriate. The Independent Price Expert’s task is to determine whether a proposed price increase is reasonable and appropriate, but may in his/her determination accept, reject or vary the proposed price increase. The Independent Price Expert would only decide to vary a proposed price increase if he/she came to his or her own view as to the appropriateness of the price increase. The considerations to which the Independent Price Expert is to have regard are not limited although he or she must have regard to the enumerated principles in cl 3.4. The considerations are extensive and include the same pricing principles that the ACCC must have regard to when determining an access dispute under Part IIIA of the CCA.

[1547] Further, although the Independent Price Expert cannot determine that PN’s access charge should decrease in a particular year, it is open to the Independent Price Expert to not allow any price increase for a year (or longer).

[1548] Further, the fact that access charges may allow multi-part pricing or price discrimination is not open ended. It is only permissible if the cost of providing the service is higher or it aids efficiency. PN is also required to endure that any multi-part pricing or price discrimination is transparent (Sch 4, cl 3.4(c)), which is also consistent with the pricing principles set out in Part IIIA of the CCA.

###### No effective protection of confidential information

[1549] The ACCC says that the Undertaking does not effectively protect the confidential information of third parties, and as a result there is a risk that PN could use information obtained from third parties in connection with their applications to use, or use of, the BMUT, to their detriment and to the advantage of PN’s own rail operations.

[1550] Clause 7.1 of the Undertaking establishes that in the event of an access application by a third party, “Pacific National must establish a separate business unit (Acacia Ridge Terminal Business Unit) that will be responsible for the management and operation of the Terminal”.

[1551] The ACCC says that this form of separation of the Acacia Ridge Terminal Business Unit (ARTBU) does not provide outcomes that are comparable to having a genuinely independent owner and operator of the ART making decisions about the Terminal. Despite the requirements of cl 7.3 that the ARTBU must deal with the PN intermodal business at arm’s length, its employees are PN employees, and are unlikely to forget that fact during their dealings with other parts of the PN business.

[1552] The ACCC says that the ineffectiveness of the regime established by cl 7 is further illustrated by the following factors:

  (a) The proposed separation of the ARTBU is incomplete; the Undertaking does not require charges for Terminal Services to be set by the ARTBU, as PN itself retains the ability to set charges.

  (b) The lack of detail in the Undertaking to ensure effectiveness of the operational separation of the business unit, for example, by structuring the roles of the terminal unit management team in a way that incentivises them to engage in arm’s length dealings with the rest of the PN business, means that the Undertaking does not adequately safeguard the confidential information of third parties.

  (c) The Undertaking permits PN to disclose confidential terminal information of an access seeker or user in limited circumstances, including to senior managers or the boards of PN in the course of ordinary reporting functions. This raises two concerns. It is inherent in this that despite the supposed structural separation of the ARTBU, there will continue to be senior managers and board members of PN that will have managerial oversight over both the ARTBU, and PN’s intermodal business. Further, whilst this information is only disclosed in aggregated format, it is possible that this disclosure would give information about PN’s downstream competitors that PN is incentivised to act on.

  (d) Terminal managers also typically have a customer facing role at the Terminal as customers are dropping off and picking up freight at the Terminal, interacting with terminal management, which leads to further difficulty in maintaining the non-disclosure of confidential information through the ring-fencing obligations set out in cls 7.4 to 7.7 of the Undertaking. The interaction with customers provides a means by which information may be disclosed to customers about the performance or business of a rival rail operator. Such disclosure would not have to be a breach of the Undertaking provisions in order for it to be extremely damaging to the business of a new entrant.

[1553] But it seems to me that the Undertaking imposes clear obligations which require the following:

  (a) PN is to establish the ARTBU, a separate body (cl 7.1) which will be responsible for “day to day operation and control of the Terminal” (cl 7.2).

  (b) The ARTBU is to keep confidential all information provided to it by a Terminal User or Applicant and not to make such information available to any PN personnel who are not engaged in certain defined activities (cl 7.4) or who are engaged in PN’s intermodal business (cl 7.5).

  (c) PN is only to use confidential information provided by a Terminal User or Applicant for a limited number of purposes (cl 7.6).

  (d) PN is to establish and maintain effective IT systems and security measures to safeguard confidential information provided to it by Terminal Users (cl 7.7).

  (e) PN is to implement an annual compliance education program for personnel involved in the day to day operation of the Terminal or PN’s intermodal business in respect of compliance with cl 7. Such program must also be given to any new employees within 30 days of their employment commencing (cl 7.13).

  (f) PN is to appoint a “Compliance Officer” who will be responsible for monitoring PN’s compliance with cl 7 (cl 7.12).

  (g) PN is to ensure that no employee who is engaged by the ARTBU directly reports to an employee of PN’s intermodal business (cl 7.18(a)).

  (h) PN is not to second or temporarily transfer any employee between the ARTBU and PN’s intermodal business (cl 7.18(b)).

  (i) PN is to ensure that no employee who has been engaged by the ARTBU and had access to the confidential information of a Terminal User or Applicant within the last 6 months is employed or engaged within 6 months with PN (cl 7.18(c)).

  (j) Finally, PN is to report any breach of cl 7 of the Undertaking to the Independent Auditor within 5 business days of becoming aware of the breach (cl 7.20).

[1554] Further, as the ARTBU would form part of PN, it is not inappropriate for some information received by the ARTBU to be disclosed to senior managers and board members of PN or its holding company. In any case, cl 7.14(c) of the Undertaking imposes a clear obligation on PN to ensure that any information provided is in a form that does not disclose any commercially sensitive information of a Terminal User or Applicant. If information could not be provided in an aggregated or anonymised fashion, then the information could not be disclosed.

[1555] Further, in order to establish this risk, the ACCC relies on the evidence of Mr Nacey that PN employees at the ART (being part of the ARTBU) deal with customers on a daily basis and that such employees might convey customer information to employees of PN’s intermodal business because both wear the “the same shirt” and “know who the boss is”. But this assumes that even though all PN employees at the ART (including forklift drivers and yard attendants) are required to carry out their work in accordance with the terms of the Undertaking (and are required to have been provided education on this by the Compliance Officer as per cl 7.13), these employees will nevertheless find ways to inform employees of PN’s intermodal business (who are also to receive such compliance education) of customer information, despite that such actions would have nothing to do with the day to day job of ARTBU employees. That is a remote possibility.

###### PN’s compliance with the Undertaking cannot be effectively monitored and enforced

[1556] The ACCC says the Undertaking contemplates three main methods by which PN’s compliance would be monitored: (a) first, by independent audits; (b) second, PN self-reporting on its compliance with KPIs; and (c) third, by other potential terminal users identifying compliance issues and initiating dispute notices. But the ACCC says that each has shortcomings that diminish their effectiveness.

[1557] The ACCC says that although the Undertaking requires PN to report on its performance against but not to comply with certain KPIs, the KPIs give no comfort that a new applicant would be treated equally to PN. The ACCC says that they are incomplete, for example, they do not measure matters such as how often terminal windows are cancelled, or containers are left behind or damaged. It says that they are crafted such that PN could comply with them despite providing itself with a higher quality service and a third party with a lower quality service, and may not reveal short-term discrimination such as discrimination causing delays to another user during a sensitive customer tender period. It says that they are to be calculated in a way that ignores a substantial volume of the services that PN would provide at the BMUT; for example, trains arriving late at the BMUT are excluded from the KPI calculations, which would exclude in the order of 56% of PN’s trains from the KPI calculations. And it says that there are no meaningful or timely consequences for PN if it fails to meet the KPIs.

[1558] Further, the ACCC points to the fact that Mr Graham Moore gave evidence that PN would not discriminate against another rail operator if it acquired the ART and the Undertaking was in place. Further, he gave evidence that he had input into the KPIs adopted under the Undertaking (under cl 8 and Schedule 3), and he considered that they would be “effective”. But the ACCC says that in cross-examination, he revealed that he is not, in fact, familiar with how the KPIs under the Undertaking operate. When asked whether there is an obligation under the Undertaking requiring PN to comply with the KPIs, Mr Graham Moore answered “That’s correct. It is. We do have to comply with the KPIs”. However the Undertaking contains no such obligation: PN’s obligation is simply to report against the KPIs and to do so on an aggregated basis. Further, the express words of the Undertaking disclaim the possibility that the KPIs might be used as a mechanism to monitor whether PN is achieving the KPIs comparably in relation to its own rail operations and those of other rail operators. Rather, Schedule 3 states that the KPIs “will not be used to measure the performance of individual terminal users”. The design of the KPIs under the Undertaking is such that a new entrant wanting to see whether it is receiving a comparable quality service to that which PN is providing to itself will be none the wiser for reading PN’s reports against the KPIs.

[1559] Further, the ACCC says that Mr Graham Moore agreed that the KPIs under the Undertaking suffered from many of the flaws that Dr Kuypers identified as being examples of how the Undertaking, and in particular the KPIs, would in fact permit PN to discriminate against another rail operator. Mr Graham Moore agreed that KPI 1, regarding trains being ready for departure, in practice would be satisfied if compliance was achieved in less than 80% of cases; he agreed that KPI 2, regarding truck turnaround times, would be ineffective in measuring instances where delays occurred and trucks were turned away from the terminal before entering the gate; and he agreed that PN could comply with KPI 3, regarding freight availability, even if PN achieved the identified target in less than 80% of cases. In addition, Mr Graham Moore agreed that there are matters that would be important to rail operators that are not addressed in the KPIs, such as how often containers are left behind or damaged.

[1560] Further, the ACCC refers to cl 9.8 of the Undertaking which provides that:

> In conducting an audit and preparing a report in accordance with clause 9.7, the Independent Auditor shall disregard any non-compliance with the Undertaking, including achievement of the KPI in Schedule 3, where such non-compliance is a result of Pacific National complying with a Rail Regulation or undertaking Works pursuant to clause 6 of this Undertaking.

[1561] The ACCC says that Dr Kuypers has provided evidence that PN’s ability to rely on rail regulations in the Undertaking allows it to impose additional safety requirements, which cause additional cost and delay, on its competitors, without breaching the Undertaking. By requiring the Independent Auditor to disregard any non-compliance with the Undertaking that results from PN complying with a Rail Regulation, the ACCC says that that provides a significant situation in which PN has the ability to discriminate against other terminal users, and for which there is no independent auditing.

[1562] Further, it is said that I cannot rely upon third parties effectively identifying contraventions of the Undertaking and then enforcing those contraventions through the specified price and non-price dispute processes.

[1563] It says that an Applicant (as defined) or new entrant will have less information about PN’s actions than PN. This information asymmetry would prevent effective enforcement of the Undertaking by an Applicant or new entrant, especially for obligations that provide PN with flexibility as to how it must act. For example, a new entrant may have suspicions that PN has been discriminating in its treatment of the new entrant’s late running trains. But the new entrant is highly unlikely to have sufficient information to be able to pursue a concern that PN has not used its “best endeavours” to accommodate those trains as it is required to do in accordance with Sch 1, cl 6(b)(ii).

[1564] Similarly, the ACCC says that any breach of the structural separation or ring-fencing requirements in cl 7 of the Undertaking would be very difficult to monitor or detect.

[1565] Let me deal with each of these submissions in turn.

[1566] Generally, I consider the ACCC’s submissions concerning the KPIs to be overstated and many of its criticisms to be of a theoretical concern rather than of practical significance.

[1567] Further, the individual KPIs have clearly not been prepared with a view to permitting PN to provide discriminatory service levels. PN could only comply with the KPIs and report on such every 6 months if it were to provide the same level of service to all users of the Terminal.

[1568] Further, the KPIs, and in particular Item 1 of Sch 3 (train ready for departure), are intended to be a meaningful measurement. By excluding late arriving trains, the KPI accurately measures whether on-time trains continue to depart on-time. To the extent that late arrivals tend to compound a train’s schedule, their inclusion in the measurement of the KPI will not accurately capture the timeliness of train departures.

[1569] Further, there was nothing of substance in the ACCC’s criticism of the percentage set for KPI 5. The objective set is commercial and otherwise unremarkable.

[1570] Further, it would seem inappropriate for an undertaking to impose a penalty on someone who has failed to comply with any KPIs included in the undertaking. Such issues should be dealt with in any terminal services agreements negotiated between the service provider and the access seeker.

[1571] In any case, the ACCC’s criticisms regarding the nature of and compliance with the KPIs are relatively minor and are unlikely to affect the decision making process of any potential entrant.

[1572] Further, the effect of cl 9.8 of the Undertaking is not to allow PN to impose additional safety requirements on its competitors. It just states that PN’s failure to comply with the Undertaking or any KPI is to be disregarded where the failure has arisen from PN’s compliance with a Rail Regulation or in undertaking capacity expansion works. In my view that is an unremarkable provision.

[1573] Further, if PN were to impose additional safety requirements in a discriminatory manner that would constitute a breach of cls 5.1 (and Sch 1, cl 2(a)) and 5.3(d) of the Undertaking, potentially attracting a sanction.

[1574] Further, as I have previously stated, the Undertaking includes a mechanism for PN to report on its own compliance (cl 8) and for the Independent Auditor to report independently on PN’s compliance (cl 9). Users are also able to request the Independent Auditor to undertake an ad-hoc compliance audit in accordance with cl 9 in relation to a specific complaint (cl 11.6).

###### Ineffective non-price dispute resolution

[1575] The ACCC says that even if third parties are able to overcome the information asymmetry so as to pursue a likely breach of the Undertaking, the specified non-price dispute resolution process still allows PN to favour its own interests during the dispute process.

[1576] The ACCC refers to the fact that the Undertaking sets out the process by which a non-price dispute may be resolved in cl 11 and Sch 5. Clause 11.2 sets out that this process applies to disputes “in relation to Pacific National’s compliance with: (a) Clause 5 – Access conditions affecting the Dispute Applicant; (b) Clause 6 – Terminal capacity; (c) Clause 7 – Access to and use of that Dispute Applicant’s ring-fenced Confidential Information (if any)”.

[1577] The ACCC says that the process for non-price dispute resolution as set out in Sch 5 has requirements of the parties that are both unclear and subject to interpretation.

[1578] First, Sch 5, cl 8 of the Undertaking defines a “Terminal Dispute” as a “bona fide commercial dispute…”. There is no guidance in the Undertaking about how someone would establish that there is a bona fide commercial dispute, or how PN should define the term. This requirement is unclear whether a dispute is capable of being referred to the non-price dispute resolution process in Sch 5.

[1579] Second, there are a number of other requirements in the process set out in Sch 5 that requires PN to “undertake genuine and good faith negotiations” (cl 3(a)), to “use all reasonable endeavours” (cls 5(a) and 5(d)), to “take all steps within its power” (cls 5(b) and 6(b)) and provide “all reasonable assistance” (cls 4.1(d)(ii) and 5(d)(ii)). The obligations in these provisions are ambiguous which may lead to further disputes about interpretation and lead to PN acting on its incentive to circumvent its obligations.

[1580] Further, the ACCC says that resolving the dispute involves cumbersome, time-consuming, costly and complex independent expert review and dispute resolution processes. It says that the non-price dispute resolution provisions in the Undertaking outline a process that takes time, expense and adds uncertainty about the ability of a new entrant to access and use the Terminal. It says that the non-price dispute resolution can be used by PN to delay a new entrant’s access to the Terminal by up to nine months.

[1581] But as to these points I would note the following.

[1582] It is incorrect to say that the non-price dispute resolution process is available only for disputes concerning cls 5, 6 or 7 of the Undertaking. Clause 11.2 of the Undertaking does not confine disputes to these clauses, but rather provides that disputes under cls 5, 6 or 7 are subject to the relevant process, “for the avoidance of doubt”.

[1583] Further, the requirement that a dispute be a “bona fide commercial dispute” is a legitimate requirement which is designed to avoid the process being utilised for trivial issues. Again, phrases such as “undertake genuine and good faith negotiations”, to “use all reasonable endeavours”, “take all steps within its power” and to provide “reasonable assistance” are familiar phrases which have well accepted meanings.

[1584] Further, the non-price dispute resolution process provides for a number of alternative means of dispute resolution with expert determination or arbitration available only if a dispute has not been resolved through one of the other mechanisms. Only if a dispute remains unresolved will the process potentially become protracted. In any case, given the long lead time associated with entry into the intermodal freight market, such delays are inconsequential. The ACCC’s criticisms of the non-price dispute resolution process are minor in nature and are unlikely to affect the decision making process of a potential new entrant.

###### Summary

[1585] Drawing the relevant threads together, in my view the Undertaking provides a suitable and useful mechanism to ensure access to the ART by operators other than PN by reasons of, in summary, the following matters.

[1586] First, it requires PN to publish a pro-forma terminal services agreement along with an application form and information in relation to access charges so that potential users can understand the obligations of each party prior to considering whether to apply for access.

[1587] Second, it contains a compulsory mechanism for PN to abide by the terms of a CAP applicable to all users of the Terminal which has been prepared by an Independent Expert. Users may then seek variations to the protocol, to be determined by the Independent Expert.

[1588] Third, it contains a compulsory mechanism for the expansion of capacity at the ART. Subject to any agreement with the access seeker, the determination of costs and other terms which apply in respect of that expansion is ultimately the subject of decision by an Independent Expert.

[1589] Fourth, the Undertaking contains provisions requiring PN to keep users’ information confidential and to set up the ARTBU that is separate from the remainder of the PN haulage business. It contains restrictions on the ARTBU employees reporting to, being seconded to or working in the PN haulage business.

[1590] Fifth, the Undertaking contains compulsory price and non-price dispute resolution mechanisms. Both are robust and lead to an ultimate determination by an Independent Expert. The Undertaking also allows a user to have the Independent Auditor carry out an ad hoc audit.

[1591] Sixth, it contains various non-discrimination obligations on PN, including an obligation not to engage in conduct which prevents or hinders users from gaining access or providing services to their customers.

[1592] Seventh, it contains various audit and compliance requirements. An auditor is appointed specifically to monitor PN’s compliance with the Undertaking and to take action in respect of any breaches.

[1593] Eighth and more generally, if PN does not come to an agreement with a user, an outcome may be imposed by one of the processes set out in the Undertaking.

[1594] Now as I have said, the ACCC complains that because the Undertaking is offered as an undertaking to the Court, that requires extensive supervision by me. But that is not correct. The Undertaking provides for obligations with which PN is required to comply. The threat of being found to have breached that Undertaking, which would be a contempt of court, is a powerful incentive for PN to comply. Further, a significant part of the Undertaking is the requirement that PN make its standard terminal services agreement terms available. Accordingly, once a terminal services agreement is entered into, the user has its usual contractual remedies.

[1595] Let me now say something more generally.

[1596] The ACCC says that the ability of a new entrant to gain access to the ART, the conditions under which they gain access, the capacity that is allocated, the confidentiality of the information provided in the course of their access application, and the ability of parties to resolve disputes efficiently are all important factors underpinning the likelihood of new entry in reliance on the Undertaking.

[1597] But it points out that Mr Nacey considers that no form of undertaking by PN could adequately address and constrain PN’s ability and incentive to restrict, delay or prevent access to the ART by any third party. He considers that PN’s ability and incentive to restrict, delay or prevent access to the ART by any new entrant, both up front and in relation to day to day operations and servicing of customers, creates such significant risks for a third party such as Qube that he could not recommend that Qube establish a business supplying Rail Services in the relevant markets if PN owned and controlled the ART, even if the Undertaking was in place. Further, Mr Nacey is not aware of PN operating any terminal on a multi-user basis that is used by a direct competitor. He is also not aware of any rail operator other than PN having access to the South Dynon terminal, which PN is required to operate subject to a regulated access regime.

[1598] Further, the ACCC says that Mr Nacey’s fears are consistent with the evidence given by Dr Kuypers. Dr Kuypers provided evidence during cross examination about gaps in the access undertaking at the South Dynon terminal such that the undertaking did not eliminate PN’s ability to discriminate. Dr Kuypers who was involved in developing the undertaking stated that the undertaking has gaps in relation to capacity allocation and “flexibility that the terminal owner has in terms of allocating capacity or determining available capacity and then allocating that capacity” and that “the terminal protocol combined with KPIs provide a reasonable amount of operational flexibility so that I wouldn’t say that they would be binding against discriminatory [conduct]”. The ACCC says that in light of this real-world example of the failings of undertakings of this kind, I should not lightly dismiss the concerns held by Mr Nacey about the Undertaking.

[1599] Indeed, the ACCC submits:

> [N]o undertaking, of any description, can remedy PN’s underlying incentive to act in its own best interests. An undertaking merely attempts to constrain the way in which that incentive can be exercised.

[1600] Now I did not find Mr Nacey’s evidence to be convincing on this question. Following his cross-examination it became apparent that his views about the Undertaking follow from his general view that obligations set out in a contract or other written document cannot provide Qube with any substantial protection.

[1601] But there are difficulties with Mr Nacey’s evidence in this respect. First, it is inconsistent with his expectation that other commercial operators will rely on the promises which Qube will make in respect of access to its Moorebank terminal. Second, his view amounts to the suggestion that the obligations contained in the Undertaking are meaningless and unlikely to be followed by PN. Following the evidence of Mr Graham Moore, it is clear that if the Undertaking is in place, PN considers that it will be obliged to comply with it. Third, Mr Nacey’s view is that PN will ignore the fact that a breach of the Undertaking may lead to contempt of court proceedings. But for PN to so ignore is inherently unlikely.

[1602] For those reasons, Mr Nacey’s evidence about these matters is problematic to say the least. The Undertaking provides meaningful and substantial protection to users and potential users of the ART.

[1603] Further, if the Undertaking were accepted, that would be an affirmative benefit of the ART acquisition going ahead, compared with the conditions of competition which would exist in the future without the ART acquisition.

[1604] First, on the evidence, no equivalent would be in place were Aurizon to remain in control of the ART.

[1605] Second, Qube has not offered to give an undertaking under s 87B to grant access to the ART to third parties if it was to acquire the ART. It could therefore refuse to grant access to the terminal altogether, including to PN once PN’s current terminal services agreement expires, or grant access on terms which the parties cannot enforce.

[1606] Further, the role of the Independent Expert within the Undertaking is significant in constraining any discriminatory conduct by PN.

[1607] Further, in respect of other terminals which PN operates, no detailed analysis has been undertaken of the reasons why other users may or may not wish to utilise PN terminals. Mr Graham Moore gave evidence that no party has made any request to use the South Dynon terminal. In light of that evidence, I agree with PN that it cannot be said that the South Dynon terminal is a “real-world example of the failings of undertakings of this kind”.

[1608] More generally concerning the future with and without analysis:

  (a) with the ART acquisition, PN will provide the Undertaking, which imposes substantial access obligations on PN should a potential entrant ever come forward; and

  (b) without the ART acquisition, the owner of the terminal (whether Aurizon or another) will have complete discretion in respect of any new user, and the various obligations contained in the Undertaking discussed earlier will not apply.

[1609] In my view that is a pro-competitive effect of the Undertaking whatever criticisms are made of it by the ACCC. Now I well appreciate the ACCC’s philosophical negativity concerning both structural and behavioural undertakings. Nevertheless, the approach of French J in AGL provides a persuasive precedent justifying my acceptance of the Undertaking.

### CONCLUSION

[1610] In summary I have rejected the ACCC’s s 45 case.

[1611] As to the ACCC’s s 50 case, upon my acceptance of the Undertaking which I consider to be part of the future with the ART acquisition, I would reject that case as well.

[1612] Now an interesting hypothetical arises as to whether, if I had not accepted the Undertaking, I would have found for the ACCC on its s 50 case. On balance, and not without some hesitation, I would have accepted its case. In the absence of the Undertaking, barriers to entry, real or reasonably perceived, would be heightened, particularly in relation to the supply of North-South Rail Services whether constituting a separate Interstate market or being part of the one Interstate market which also includes East-West Rail Services. On that basis I do not need to concern myself with Aurizon’s submission that the ART acquisition would not on any view affect the supply of East-West Rail Services. Now although Qube was not likely to enter in the factual or the counterfactual, the latter with the TSS, I could not rule out the realistic commercial chance of another potential new entrant emerging in the relevant timeframe. Further, the potential threat of such emergence could act as a discipline on PN’s present behaviour concerning the supply of North-South Rail Services. But given that I have accepted the Undertaking, I do not need to elaborate further. And nor do I need to hypothesise further concerning the effect on any market for rail linehaul services concerning bulk steel if I had not accepted the Undertaking.

[1613] I will give the parties an opportunity to make submissions concerning final orders including on questions of confidentiality and costs.

I certify that the preceding 1613 (one thousand six hundred and thirteen) numbered paragraphs are a true copy of the Reasons for Judgment herein of the Honourable Justice Beach.

Associate:

Dated: 15 May 2019
